What is Climate Change

CrashCourse, 08 15, 2023

Video (13:57)

Description

Life on Earth has weathered boiling-hot oceans and volcanic-ash-darkened skies—but that’s nothing like the climate change we’re experiencing now. In this episode of Crash Course Biology, we’ll talk about the greenhouse effect, learn why our climate is like a tangled pair of headphones, and discover that we’ve understood the science behind climate change for much longer than you might think.

Chapters:

Introduction to Climate Change 00:00

The Greenhouse Effect 2:24

Measuring the Greenhouse Effect 4:46

Carbon Sinks 8:26

Environmental Justice 10:07

Review & Credits 12:09

This series was produced in collaboration with HHMI BioInteractive, committed to empowering educators and inspiring students with engaging, accessible, and quality classroom resources. Visit https://BioInteractive.org/CrashCourse for more information.

Are you an educator looking for what NGSS Standards are covered in this episode? Check out our Educator Standards Database for Biology here: https://www.thecrashcourse.com/biologystandards

Sources: https://docs.google.com/document/d/1GLDtAXE6ekg4Chk2qN3TYbNt0pJbyaHqTqRd6QY8pd4/edit?usp=sharing

Transcript

Introduction to Climate Change

0:00 · Our planet has been draped in ice sheets, filled with boiling-hot oceans, and dimmed by volcanic ash – all before anything more complicated than a single-celled organism showed up.

0:10 · So climate change is nothing new around here… when it happens gradually, over millions of years.

0:17 · But something new has happened in the last few centuries.

0:20 · People like us…except, wearing hats like this… began burning fossil fuels like coal, oil, and natural gas to make energy.

0:29 · Most of us aren’t wearing hats like those anymore.

0:31 · But we’re still powering our daily lives and industries with those fuels, releasing billions of tons of carbon dioxide every year.

0:41 · And that’s caused Earth’s climate to change in the span of just a few human lifetimes, the geological blink of an eye.

0:48 · Hi! I’m Dr. Sammy, your friendly neighborhood entomologist, and this is Crash Course Biology!

0:56 · Hey, do you guys smell that?

0:58 · …smells like…

1:00 · theme music?

1:01 · [THEME MUSIC] Now, I know what you’re thinking.

1:12 · “Wait, isn’t this Crash Course Biology?”

1:15 · And yeah, this episode is heavy on the gases, low on the golgi bodies.

1:19 · And that might leave you wondering, what does climate change have to do with the science of life?

1:25 · Here’s the thing: life and climate are tied together, like that tangled pair of headphones at the bottom of your backpack.

1:30 · You know, like you tug one end and then a knot tightens, which is looped around a paperclip, somehow snagged on that tiny notebook where you drew hearts around your crush’s name, which after several tugs is now lying open on the floor and…I’m breaking into a cold sweat just thinking about it.

1:46 · But back to the point, we can’t talk about life without talking about climate —which doesn’t mean last week’s thunderstorm or a one-day temperature swing – that’s weather.

1:58 · Climate is long-term weather conditions averaged over many years.

2:03 · To understand the difference between them, just remember that knowing the weather will help you decide if you should grab an umbrella before you head out, but knowing the climate will help you decide if you should invest in a good air conditioner.

2:15 · While the weather might impact your choice of clothing for the day, the climate directly impacts where and when different kinds of life can survive.

The Greenhouse Effect

2:24 · We owe today’s climate to the fact that our little green-and-blue marble of a home isn’t just floating in space unprotected.

2:31 · It’s wrapped up in an atmosphere; a big, invisible, gassy jacket.

2:35 · Which, granted, sounds pretty weird when you put it that way.

2:38 · But without it, we wouldn’t exist.

2:40 · This jacket is made of different kinds of gases.

2:43 · And a small fraction of them, known as greenhouse gases, absorb solar energy like, super well.

2:49 · We’re talking about gases like methane, water vapor, and most importantly carbon dioxide – also known as CO2.

2:58 · They account for less than half of one percent of our atmospheric jacket.

3:03 · But they’re a part of what makes it so good at trapping heat, sort of like all the little white feathers in your puffy coat.

3:09 · When sunlight beams down from space, most of it travels through those gases with no problem.

3:14 · The energy from that sunlight gets absorbed as it strikes the Earth, warming the surface.

3:19 · That type of warming is normal and seasonal.

3:22 · The Earth naturally bounces some of that solar energy back toward space.

3:27 · Where some of it exits the atmosphere and heads right back out into the inky ether.

3:32 · But the rest of that energy gets trapped by the Earth’s gassy jacket.

3:36 · Specifically by those super absorbent greenhouse gases, which suck up heat and bounce it back down to us again.

3:44 · This warming process is called the greenhouse effect.

3:47 · Unsurprisingly, It works the same way as a greenhouse – using layers of glass to trap heat inside.

3:54 · And it keeps Earth at a nice, cozy, insulated average of 14 degrees Celsius.

4:00 · Without it, our Earth would be a chilly average of -18 degrees Celsius.

4:06 · Great for storing ice cream!

4:08 · Not so good for rainforests, swimsuits, or us for that matter.

4:13 · So, the greenhouse effect is a natural, helpful process that makes Earth habitable for all of life!

4:18 · But you can have too much of a good thing.

4:21 · When our atmospheric jacket contains more carbon dioxide, for example, it gets really good at trapping heat.

4:28 · Like, too good.

4:30 · And the hotter things get, the more water evaporates and joins the atmosphere— and remember, water vapor itself is a greenhouse gas, so that in turn absorbs even more heat, creating a looping system of cause-and-effect that just keeps reinforcing itself.

Measuring the Greenhouse Effect

4:46 · And while dressing in layers is great if you’re hiking in the Alps, it’s really hard for our planet to shed its extra coats.

4:52 · So all of that heat gets stuck going from the ground to the atmosphere like the worst game of hot potato ever played.

4:59 · When I learned about this, I was like, “Wow, what a revelation!

5:03 · I can’t believe we’ve only recently figured this out!”

5:05 · But it turns out that this isn’t new knowledge; we’ve known how, and why, this could happen for nearly 170 years.

5:16 · Let’s pay a visit to the Theater of Life… [Inquisitive music] Back in 1856, Eunice Foote, an American scientist and suffragette, was thinking about how the Sun’s warmth affected different gases.

5:27 · In those days, the scientific community was a bit like a fort with a handmade “no girls allowed” sign out front.

5:34 · But Foote wasn’t deterred, and ran her experiments anyway.

5:38 · She filled tubes with different combinations of gases, including carbon dioxide.

5:42 · After putting some tubes in the Sun and some in the shade, she compared their temperatures, trying to find the hottest gas.

5:50 · All the tubes in direct sunlight warmed up.

5:52 · But none as intensely as the tube that contained carbon dioxide.

5:57 · The temperature had soared to 51.7 degrees Celsius, hot enough to burn your fingers.

6:04 · From that insight, Foote theorized that if our atmosphere ever contained more carbon dioxide, the whole planet would warm up as a result, which would mean a lot more than a few scalded fingers.

6:16 · And sure enough, today, that’s exactly the situation we’re in.

6:20 · Foote was one of the first scientists to recognize carbon dioxide’s potential to affect Earth’s climate.

6:25 · But she wasn’t the only one to connect the dots.

6:28 · For example, just a few decades later, another scientist named Dr. Svante Arrhenius observed that burning coal releases carbon dioxide.

6:37 · As a fossil fuel, coal forms from the decomposed, carbon-based bodies of plants and animals that lived and died a long time ago.

6:46 · And I’m talking before the dinosaurs.

6:48 · So no, I’m afraid that means you aren’t gassing up your car with the remains of a T-Rex.

6:53 · When we burn those fossil fuels— whether it’s in the form of coal, oil, or natural gas— carbon dioxide gets released into the atmosphere.

7:00 · When Arrhenius ran the numbers, he predicted that carbon dioxide released by burning fossil fuels could warm our climate within a few thousand years if we kept burning them at their current rate.

7:14 · But we’ve far outpaced his estimates.

7:16 · Our emissions of carbon dioxide have grown and grown and grown — and so has the mountain of evidence that those emissions are warming our planet.

7:25 · Some oil and gas companies have worked to promote uncertainty around the existence of climate change and what’s causing it.

7:32 · While they have only very recently acknowledged its existence, as of 2023, they’re still trying to deflect from what’s causing it.

7:40 · But the scientific consensus on this is overwhelming.

7:44 · You can learn much more about that in our Climate & Energy series.

7:47 · We’ve only been reliably taking direct measurements of Earth’s global temperature since the 19th century.

7:52 · But that measly slice of time shows a steady rise in temperature of about 1.1 degree Celsius since 1880.

8:01 · And we know that that’s unusual because we’ve learned to read Earth’s much longer climate diary, in the form of ice cores.

8:07 · See, when snow hardens into ice, tiny bubbles of air remain trapped in the gaps between snowflakes.

8:13 · These gases and water molecules stay frozen, like entries in a frosty journal.

8:19 · So by drilling deep down into polar ice, we can snoop on what the atmosphere was like hundreds of thousands of years ago.

Carbon Sinks

8:27 · Ice cores show us that carbon dioxide levels have fluctuated over the past 800,000 years.

8:32 · And temperatures have fallen and risen alongside them, too.

8:36 · But when people started burning fossil fuels, carbon dioxide levels began to spike quickly, like really, really quickly.

8:44 · And they haven’t stopped rising.

8:45 · Before the Industrial Revolution, for every million molecules of air in our atmosphere, around 280 were carbon dioxide molecules.

8:54 · But by 2022, that number had increased to 422 —the highest concentration of CO2 our planet has seen in 4 million years.

9:06 · And that surge in carbon dioxide affects more than just the temperature.

9:10 · As the amount of carbon dioxide in our atmosphere rises, it impacts all of Earth’s systems.

9:15 · Just like yanking on the end of that tangled mess of headphones impacted all of the other items in my bag (and eventually, everybody around us when stuff started falling out).

9:24 · It’s all connected, is what I’m saying.

9:26 · For example, the ocean is our planet’s largest carbon sink.

9:31 · It’s sort of like a big storage container for carbon.

9:34 · In fact, the ocean holds 50 times more carbon than the air or soil do.

9:40 · But a chemical reaction happens when carbon dioxide meets water, it creates an acid.

9:45 · So that influx of carbon has already turned the ocean 30% more acidic since the 19th century.

9:53 · As carbon dioxide and other greenhouse gases trap more heat, there’s more energy pouring into our planet than going out.

9:59 · That means more energy is pumped into the ocean, fueling hurricanes and typhoons to become more frequent and more intense.

Environmental Justice

10:07 · As the whole planet gets hotter, that triggers all kinds of changes.

10:11 · Spring arrives earlier, leading to shorter winters and longer summers.

10:15 · Rising temperatures lead to double-whammy droughts and heatwaves.

10:19 · And that leaves forest floors full of dried-up plants that serve as fuel for wildfires to ignite— spreading faster, farther, and more often.

10:28 · Plus, Earth’s polar ice caps are melting, transforming solid ice to slush and seawater.

10:34 · And that’s causing ocean levels to rise and encroach on land.

10:38 · So while some communities are already facing a problem of not enough water, others are facing a problem of too much.

10:45 · And because these adverse changes layer on top of existing social inequalities, they disproportionately affect lower-income communities and people of color, making climate change not only a scientific issue, but a matter of environmental justice that has spurred some researchers to political action.

11:02 · For example, climate scientist Nicole Hernandez Hammer witnessed first-hand, through her field research, the sea-level rise alongside Miami Beach’s Latino communities.

11:12 · But these communities were not included in conversations about climate change or the dangers of rising sea levels.

11:18 · So, Hammer took action, moving into environmental outreach and education.

11:23 · And, while the threat is still there, these communities are now in conversation about climate change, and able to plan and advocate for the future of their environment.

11:33 · And the good news more broadly is we know exactly why these tangled, complex effects are happening.

11:39 · The more we burn those fossil fuels, the more greenhouse gases we release —and that’s driving sweeping changes all over our planet.

11:47 · And these changes are impacting life at all levels from the tiniest bacterium, to the elephants of Botswana, to you and me.

11:55 · We can slow these impacts by breaking up with fossil fuels.

11:58 · But we also have to invest in nature’s carbon sinks —such as soils and forests— which pull carbon out of the atmosphere and back into the land, where it can’t keep heating things up.

Review & Credits

12:09 · It won’t be the easiest breakup.

12:12 · We’ve designed whole societies and global systems around fossil fuels.

12:16 · We should fully expect to be listening to Jazmine Sullivan on repeat with a gallon of Rocky Road.

12:21 · But this is one relationship we can’t afford to stay in.

12:24 · So, in order to halt emissions, we have to both invent new systems and work more efficiently within old ones, and to do that, we’ve got to get a whole planet’s worth of people on board.

12:35 · And if you’ve ever been part of a group project, you know that last part isn’t easy.

12:39 · Thankfully, when faced with global crises, humans have one great thing going for us: we are creative.

12:46 · I mean, we’ve been to space, we’ve got electric cars, we made furbies for some reason.

12:53 · So yes, It’s going to take all of our creativity and cooperation to tackle.

12:58 · But the only way out is through, and the only way through is together.

13:04 · In our next episode, we’ll tune back into the world of living things— and see how our rapidly changing climate involves much more than the atmosphere.

13:12 · It affects every living, breathing, organism on this planet— including you and me.

13:18 · This series was produced in collaboration with HHMI BioInteractive.

13:22 · If you’re an educator, visit BioInteractive.org/CrashCourse for classroom resources and professional development related to the topics covered in this course.

13:32 · Thanks for watching this episode of Crash Course Biology, which was filmed at our studio in Indianapolis, Indiana, and was made with the help of all these nice people.

13:41 · If you want to help keep Crash Course free for everyone, forever, you can join our community on Patreon.

World Weather Attribution — understanding climate change and extreme weather

World Weather Attribution, 11 13, 2023

Video (5:59)

Description

When an extreme weather event occurs, people often ask: is climate change to blame?

For decades, climate scientists have answered that question in general terms – that as the planet warms, we can expect many of these weather events to become more frequent and extreme.

But in recent years, advances in attribution science have allowed researchers to answer the question in much greater detail.

Founded in 2015 by Dr. Friederike Otto and Dr. Geert Jan van Oldenborgh, World Weather Attribution scientists quantify how climate change influences the intensity and likelihood of an extreme weather event. They often do this using weather data and computer modelling, and often immediately in the aftermath of the extreme event.

To encourage actions that will make communities and countries more resilient to future extreme weather events, World Weather Attribution studies also evaluate how existing vulnerability worsened the impacts of the extreme weather event.

To learn more about World Weather Attribution or to view our studies, go to https://www.worldweatherattribution.org/

Follow us on Twitter: https://twitter.com/WWAttribution

Film produced by Geoff Marsh.

Transcript

Introduction to attribution

0:03 · [Music] in the immediate aftermath of an extreme weather event occurring people always ask the question is climate change to blame we found it World weather attribution to answer that question based on scientific evidence this one does not include the EV weather attribution is a relatively

0:26 · new field of science that basically brings together weather obervation and meteorology and climate science and really answers the question what is the cause of an event happening I’m Dr Freddy Oto I’m a senior lecturer at the Grandam Institute at Imperial College London and I am co-founder of the world weather attribution initiative which I’m also leading our burning of fossil fuels for

Climate change impacts

0:56 · um the last decades and Century has led to the world over all getting warmer and that had a profound effect on weather around the world so every heat wave that is happening today has been made more likely more intense and longer because

1:12 · of climate change but we also see changes in other weather events heavy rainfall has become more intense in many parts of the world we see more intense droughts or at least the impacts of droughts on agriculture are worse as they would have been without climate change because of the increase in temperature and of course that doesn’t affect just the weather but our societies are only adapted to a very

1:39 · small range of possible weather and we are outside this range now and therefore the impacts that these changes have are that people lose their lives their livelihoods across the world not just in the global South but everywhere in the

1:58 · world so the kind of extreme weather events that we would want to St study with World weather attribution are very frequent so um we meet every Monday with our team and look at the events that have happened and there are many more events happening than we can study and we are aiming to look at about 20 uh per

Case studies and examples

2:22 · year so the kind of thing we are looking at are events like for example the Pacific Northwest Heatwave that hit the US in Canada in 2021 where we found this event would have basically been impossible without climate change another event that has not been talked about much in the global media was extreme flooding in Nigeria and around the Nija Delta more widely that happened in 2022 where we also found that climate

2:48 · change made this event 80 times more likely which is a huge change for a rainfall event so with our core team we decide whether we can do a study based Bas on whether we have enough manpower available whether we have data and

The research process

3:05 · climate models at least in principle that are able to simulate the type of event whether we have the ability to make contact with people in the region and also we try um to look at is it a kind of event in a region where we have already done many many studies if yes we might give that a lower priority but if it’s in a region where not many Studies have been done we will definitely try to do it so once we have made contact with the

3:34 · people in country we decide on how to define the event and then based on how complex the weather event is that led to the impacts we then decide on the timeline so if it’s a heat wve that is relatively straightforward or a heavy rainfall event we try and finish the study within a week if it’s something more complex like a drought um or

4:00 · maybe a a less straightforward rainfall event than it might take us a few weeks but we we really aim to be as fast as possible so that our evidence can still feed into the public discussion that is happening about the event so what we are aiming to do with each of these studies is to provide on

Reporting and implications

4:24 · the one hand an answer of what’s the role of climate change so something like this event has been made 80 times more likely because of climate change and on the other hand information on who has been most affected and why so who is hit

4:41 · by this weather event and why and how are they affected and also what can be done to prevent these people being affected next time so once we finish a study we obviously can’t send it to peer review and wait for 2 years until the review process is over before publicizing the events we publicize them straight away so we publish a scientific report that has all the information that a scientific paper has on our website we write a summary we write a press release

5:12 · and we do a press briefing and in many cases we then send the scientific report a few weeks after to peerreview um so that it will become a peer-reviewed paper down the line it’s important to have attribution studies because these studies show us how climate change manifests in our daily lives in our

5:35 · daily experiences not just climate scientists but everyone and also because we have an assessment on the vulnerability we can learn from these studies how to better prevent impacts to be not as dramatic for future [Music] events

Assigned Background

A Storm is Brewing - Is it Climate Change?

A Storm is Brewing. Is it Climate Change?

Duration: 00:32:29

Released: 2022

Series: How to Save a Planet

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

For years, we were told it wasn’t possible to link specific weather events to global warming — and that made communicating about climate change difficult: When extreme weather events were in the news, climate change was often left out of the story. This week, we tell the story of how that changed. We talk to the scientists who figured out how to model the role of climate change in events like Hurricane Harvey — the climate detectives who blew the case of extreme weather wide open.Guests: Dr. Peter Stott, UK Met Office; Dr. Friederike (Fredi) Otto, Grantham Institute for Climate Change and the Environment; Dr. Stephanie Herring, NOAACalls to Action: Help out climate scientists from your couch! Climate scientists rely on accurate weather data to produce and test their models. But lots of historical weather data is still locked away in handwritten records from the 1800s, like old ships’ logs. Now, you can help rescue that data — by transcribing old temperature and weather logs! Check out the project Weather Rescue, which connects volunteers with documents that need transcribing. Especially good if you’re a pro at deciphering cursive handwriting… Talk about the weather! If an extreme weather event linked to climate change occurs in your region, talk to your friends and family about how climate change is affecting the weather they’re experiencing. One goal of scientists like Fredi Otto is to help get climate science into the conversation — and that only works if people HAVE that conversation. People like you! Further Reading: You can find more information and recent studies on extreme weather events online at World Weather Attribution Check out Dr. Fredi Otto’s book about the development of rapid event attribution, Angry Weather, or Dr. Peter Stott’s memoir about his experiences as a climate scientist, Hot Air The American Meteorological Society publishes an annual round-up of the most interesting extreme weather event attribution studies, edited by Drs. Stephanie Herring and Peter Stott. If you liked this episode, you might like some of these past shows: Unnatural Disasters and How TV Weathercasters Went from Climate Skeptics to Champions. If you take an action we recommend in one of our episodes, do us a favor and tell us about it! We’d love to hear how it went and what it felt like. Record a short voice memo on your phone and send it to us via our Listener Mail Form. We might use it in an upcoming episode. Find all the actions we’ve recommended on our show here!Credits:How to Save a Planet is a Spotify original podcast and Gimlet production. A call for redesigning American streets

Duration: 00:21:09

Released: 2018

Series: Better Off

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

In this week’s podcast we’ll explore how America’s streets can be redesigned to benefit bicyclists and pedestrians—and we’ll explain why doing so may even help mitigate the effects of climate change. A new study from Anne Lusk, research scientist in the Department of Nutrition at Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health, analyzed how bicyclists and pedestrians responded to various placements of trees along cycle tracks—barrier-protected bicycle-exclusive paths between the road and the sidewalk. The research shows that the placement of these trees can play an important role in how people walking and biking perceive traffic, pollution, heat, and even safety. But the benefits extend beyond just those using the street—and could even be a factor in mitigating the effects of climate change. These trees may make it more likely that people will bike—helping to reduce pollution. And increased greenery can also help cool cities, which often suffer from a heat island effect, which means they’re significantly warmer than more rural areas. Lusk says all of this highlights the need to re-think how we’re designing streets to encourage more people to walk and bike instead of drive.

A Storm is Brewing - Is it Climate Change?

A Storm is Brewing. Is it Climate Change?

Duration: 00:32:29

Released: 2022

Series: How to Save a Planet

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

For years, we were told it wasn’t possible to link specific weather events to global warming — and that made communicating about climate change difficult: When extreme weather events were in the news, climate change was often left out of the story. This week, we tell the story of how that changed. We talk to the scientists who figured out how to model the role of climate change in events like Hurricane Harvey — the climate detectives who blew the case of extreme weather wide open.Guests: Dr. Peter Stott, UK Met Office; Dr. Friederike (Fredi) Otto, Grantham Institute for Climate Change and the Environment; Dr. Stephanie Herring, NOAACalls to Action: Help out climate scientists from your couch! Climate scientists rely on accurate weather data to produce and test their models. But lots of historical weather data is still locked away in handwritten records from the 1800s, like old ships’ logs. Now, you can help rescue that data — by transcribing old temperature and weather logs! Check out the project Weather Rescue, which connects volunteers with documents that need transcribing. Especially good if you’re a pro at deciphering cursive handwriting… Talk about the weather! If an extreme weather event linked to climate change occurs in your region, talk to your friends and family about how climate change is affecting the weather they’re experiencing. One goal of scientists like Fredi Otto is to help get climate science into the conversation — and that only works if people HAVE that conversation. People like you! Further Reading: You can find more information and recent studies on extreme weather events online at World Weather Attribution Check out Dr. Fredi Otto’s book about the development of rapid event attribution, Angry Weather, or Dr. Peter Stott’s memoir about his experiences as a climate scientist, Hot Air The American Meteorological Society publishes an annual round-up of the most interesting extreme weather event attribution studies, edited by Drs. Stephanie Herring and Peter Stott. If you liked this episode, you might like some of these past shows: Unnatural Disasters and How TV Weathercasters Went from Climate Skeptics to Champions. An Expert Vegetable Breeder On Innovating Crops For The Future

Duration: 00:17:33

Released: 2024

Series: Science Friday

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

It’s become clear to farmers and home gardeners alike that climate change is affecting the gardening landscape, literally. The climate is warming, pests are moving into different regions, and there’s a growing need for vegetable varieties that are resilient to the stresses of this new age.In the world of organic farming, the job of creating those new varieties falls to a plant breeder: someone who, often painstakingly, crosses plants until they create a new variety. Dr. Jim Myers, one of the most accomplished plant breeders in the country, has lots of experience with this.Myers created the Indigo Rose tomato, a strikingly purple variety with the same antioxidants as blueberries. He also created the green bean cultivar predominantly used by Oregon producers, and is debuting two new varieties of low-heat habanero peppers next month, dubbed “Mild Thing” and “Notta Hotta.”Myers joins Ira Flatow from Corvallis, Oregon, where he’s a professor of agricultural science at Oregon State University. They discuss his decades-long career in plant breeding and what he sees as the biggest challenges for the plant breeders of the future. Subscribe to this podcast. Are extreme wildfires the new normal?

Duration: 00:13:21

Released: 2026

Series: Short Wave

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

With parts of the U.S, Canada, Spain and France all ablaze with wildfires, you might be wondering: Are wildfires on the rise? If so, know that you mind-melded with the entire Short Wave staff. We had the exact same thought – and then went down some reporting rabbit holes and turned it into a whole episode. And let us just say: The answer is surprisingly complicated! But don’t you worry. We get into all the nuances this episode.Interested in more science behind the headlines? REGINA BARBER: Hey, Short Wavers. It’s Gina here with a request– follow us on whichever app you’re listening from. We love sharing cool and important science stories with you. And following us means you’ll always know when our next episode is ready, just for you. And guess what. The next episode is ready.

[MUSIC PLAYING]

EMILY KWONG: You’re listening to Short Wave from NPR.

BARBER: Hello, Short Wavers, Regina Barber here with my co host, NPR science correspondent Nate Rott. Hey, Nate.

NATHAN ROTT: Hello, Gina.

BARBER: So, Nate, over the years, you’ve reported a ton on wildfires and fought some in a previous life, I heard.

ROTT: Yes, a previous, younger, more lithe life. Yes, that is correct.

BARBER: Yeah. We’re all getting old, Nate.

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: And Nate, it seems like a lot of the world is experiencing them right now. Like, fires are happening in France and Spain. Canada and even Oregon is fighting dozens of fires right now. So, with all of your expertise, riddle me this.

ROTT: OK.

BARBER: Is it my imagination, or are wildfires increasing globally?

ROTT: Gina, are you sitting down?

BARBER: I am, actually. You can see me.

ROTT: OK, yeah, I can. And you are sitting. I just wanted to confirm for anybody who can’t see you. I say, are you sitting, because overall, there are fewer wildfires globally and less land burning on average than in the past. But–

BARBER: Huh.

ROTT: –within those two sentences, Gina, there is so much nuance, and so, we’ll get into that.

ZEKE HAUSFATHER: There’s a lot of different regional trends that are underneath that.

ROTT: Yeah, so that’s Zeke Hausfather. He’s a climate scientist in California. And he says that, for example, in North American forests, like where we are, the trend is definitely more burning.

HAUSFATHER: The typical fire burns about three times more area than it did 30 years ago or 40 years ago, which is largely driven by changes in conditions. The vegetation is drier, and so when a spark catches, it’s more likely to burn a larger area.

ROTT: And Gina, globally, extreme wildfire activity has more than doubled in the last two decades.

BARBER: That’s so much.

ROTT: Yeah, it’s a lot, which means that in certain areas, there are more fires burning bigger and hotter, which, in some cases, means more smoke from those fires can travel a very long way and make a beautiful sunset, but muddy up a perfectly normal vacation.

BARBER: Yeah, which is why we’ve been seeing smoke here on the East Coast, right?

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: And one thing I’ve been wondering when it gets hazy, especially having a kid, is, like, how much time can I spend outside? And should I even be outside at all? Like, I know that there’s, like, an air quality index, but the guidelines seem vague.

ROTT: [CHUCKLES] Vague is a very diplomatic way of putting it, Gina. And–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –there’s a reason for that. You know, scientists just don’t have a lot of long-term data on wildfire smoke exposure to give people definitive guidelines, which leaves a lot of people like you and me wondering how to go about their days when there’s a lot of smoke in the air.

[MUSIC PLAYING]

BARBER: So today on the show, what to do when the world’s on fire.

ROTT: We talk about the changing wildfire landscape, what’s driving the trends, and what you can do about it.

BARBER: You’re listening to Short Wave, the science podcast from NPR.

BARBER: OK, Nate, so you said earlier that wildfires are actually decreasing globally. This is just wild to me. Like, how is that possible?

ROTT: Yeah, I mean, OK, so when we say wildfires, that includes lots of different types of environments and vegetation– forests, yes, but also, you know, grasslands and shrublands and savanna. And as anyone who messed around with fireworks as a kid or had a little pyro streak maybe like me, you will know that fire burns a lot faster and quicker in lighter vegetation, like grasses, than it does in, like, a forest, right?

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: So most of the area–

BARBER: Yeah, definitely.

ROTT: –that burns every year are grasslands, shrublands, and savannas, not forests.

BARBER: OK.

ROTT: And so, the reason that, globally, there’s less land burned is largely because there’s less grasslands and shrublands and savannas burning mostly in Africa, which, you know, is a really big continent. So that brings down the global scale.

BARBER: I love this. This is, like, fascinating, these nuances. OK, so, now, there’s these two pretty bad forest fires in France and Spain. And–

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: –outside of these fires, is Europe another place where there are fewer fires recently?

ROTT: Well, honestly, Gina, this is, like, a really active area of research, like what are the trends in wildfire are in Europe. But when our producer–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –Berly McCoy spoke to Zeke Monday about the current fires, he said the area burned was higher than average for the European Union, so–

BARBER: OK.

ROTT: –not record-setting yet. Of course, that could change. But he said these fires are understandably getting a lot of media attention because there are two big fires burning at the same time near a lot of–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –people. So it might seem like a trend, but it might not necessarily be.

BARBER: So, I mean, could this be just, like, a normal fire year for Europe? I remember, like, four years ago, a big fire in Spain. Like, is this–

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: –just normal?

ROTT: Yeah, I mean, look, it could be. But, you know, Zeke pointed out that there is a trend that is related to many wildfires today in Europe and North America everywhere. The fires that are burning in Spain and France both coincided with an extreme heat wave.

BARBER: OK, I see where this is going.

ROTT: Yeah, climate change– don’t know if you’ve heard of it, small thing–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –happening globally.

BARBER: Yeah, the climate crisis, it’s like this cartoon villain, right? It’s very predictable.

ROTT: Yeah, well, unlike a generic cartoon villain, you know, it’s not easily vanquished by Captain Planet and his gang of trusted friends.

BARBER: Yes.

ROTT: Zeke says that rising temperatures nurture fires that might otherwise just die out. So, like, take Canada, for example.

HAUSFATHER: So, every one degree centigrade, or roughly two degrees Fahrenheit, of warming during the fire season in Canada– so May to September– is associated with an 80% increase in the amount of area burned that year.

BARBER: 80%– that’s a huge jump for just, like, a couple of degrees Fahrenheit.

ROTT: Yeah, it’s massive. And he says, since the 1960s, the fire season has warmed up about four degrees Fahrenheit on average. So–

BARBER: Wow.

ROTT: –that translates to a huge amount of burned area in Canada because when it’s warmer, things just dry out quicker, so there’s literally more fuel for the fire.

BARBER: So doesn’t this also have to do with humans not doing, like, prescribed burns to clear the underbrush?

ROTT: In the United States, yes, that’s definitely true. So, in many parts of the country, not just the Western US, forests are adapted to fire. In many cases, they need it ecologically. And, you know, Native communities used fires for centuries to forests, but since at least the 1930s, we’ve been suppressing forest fires, putting them out as quickly as we can.

BARBER: I mean, Nate, only you can prevent forest fires.

ROTT: Yes, God bless Smokey Bear. It’s not Smokey the Bear, Gina. It’s Smokey Bear. Don’t let the Forest Service come after you.

BARBER: I had no idea, honestly.

ROTT: Yeah. And look, like, Smokey’s slogan was great to protect people and homes and, you know, maybe some timber, but it’s not so great to keep fuel levels down in those forests.

HAUSFATHER: When fires do occur in those forests, there’s so much undergrowth that’s accumulated that the fires are much more catastrophic. They reach up into the canopy of the trees. They become what we call crown fires that actually kill the trees–

BARBER: Geez.

HAUSFATHER: –rather than what historically has happened in Western forests, where you have these low-level fires that kill the undergrowth, but don’t reach up and actually kill the trees. The boreal forest is a different beast.

ROTT: Yeah, and the boreal forests in Canada, the ones that are smoking up parts of the US right now, those have never needed prescribed burning in the same way.

HAUSFATHER: The boreal forest–

BARBER: Wow.

HAUSFATHER: –in Canada is 4 and 1/2 times the size of Texas. And so, historically–

BARBER: What?

HAUSFATHER: –when fires burn in those forests, they’re just allowed to burn themselves out. You know, they haven’t had the same history of fire suppression that we have in the US that has built up fuel.

ROTT: So what he’s kind of saying is, it is normal for these big fires to happen in Canada, but what’s not normal is how often these huge fires are happening.

BARBER: So, historically, how often is normal for them to burn?

ROTT: So, typically, in Canada’s western boreal forest, Zeke says big fires would happen, you know, once every 50 to 200 years.

BARBER: That is not happening right now.

ROTT: No, it is much more frequent. And–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –when we’re talking about boreal forests, you know, for you non-ecology nerds out there, you know, we’re talking about these dense forests that stretch across the Northern Hemisphere, and temperatures are rising at higher latitudes faster than they are closer to the equator.

HAUSFATHER: What we’re seeing now is considerably more frequent fires, because these areas that used to be much wetter– you know, much of the the northern boreal resembles a swamp– are now much drier because of higher temperatures.

ROTT: And it’s not necessarily that more fires are starting, you know, which, in both the US and Canada, is often caused by lightning, sometimes caused by people. It’s that a warmer climate is creating the conditions for fires to get going and burn a lot more vegetation when they do.

BARBER: OK, so I grew up in the Pacific Northwest, and there weren’t a lot of wildfires. And then I had my own kid, and then wildfires and smoke were just part of the summers. And honestly, I thought I was going to just, like, avoid all that and move to the East Coast.

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: And once I’m here, I’m not going to have any of that smoke.

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: But for listeners in the same boat as me– like, now, I’m inhaling this smoke– like, what are the recommendations for staying healthy?

ROTT: Well, so the biggest piece of advice I could give you, Gina, is to monitor the air quality to figure out your exposure to risk. So, in the US–

BARBER: OK.

ROTT: –there’s an air quality index for how polluted–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –the air is in a certain area. You know, it’s based on how many pollutants are in the air, and that includes particulate matter, these tiny little particles that are so small, they can get into your bloodstream.

BARBER: Oh, my gosh.

ROTT: You know, there’s been a lot of research that shows that it’s particularly risky for pregnant people. So I’d highly recommend people use AQI as kind of a quick pulse check to figure out how good the air quality is. It goes from green, where the air pollution isn’t a risk to people, all the way up to maroon, which means it’s a risk for everybody. And there is limited guidance for, you know, when you should limit outdoor activity, for example. But Lisa Patel, a pediatrician and environmental scientist at Stanford Medicine Children’s Health, says it may not be a good enough tool for wildfires.

LISA PATEL: These air quality guidelines were built off the burning of fossil fuels. There was a study that was done on children between zero to five that found that wildfire smoke is about 10 times as toxic. 10 times as many children showed up to the emergency room with respiratory complaints.

ROTT: And it’s not just kids, right? Like, inhaling that smoke can lead to inflammation, which is linked to cancer, to heart and lung disease, and to dementia.

BARBER: Oof. So, what else can we do to limit our exposure to wildfire smoke?

ROTT: Yeah, so spending more time inside, you know, though, I should say, you know, staying inside won’t totally keep you from being exposed, especially in older homes with a lot of drafts. But, you know, staying inside is one piece of advice– wearing a mask when you go outside, recirculating the air in your car, you know, so you’re not sucking air directly from outside into the car. And using an air filter indoors goes a long way. Lisa says you can make one with a HEPA filter duct taped to a box fan. I’ve actually been to places where they’re doing this in Missoula, where I grew up. They are creating them for people that can’t afford air filters in their house. And when it comes to this bigger, you know, staying inside part that we’re talking about, Lisa knows that advice might be tough to hear.

PATEL: I’m a parent myself. My kids were 2 and 5 when– and we were in the middle of a pandemic. What we are in is a situation where– and it is sad for me to say. I grieve about it when I have to say it, but we’re increasingly having periods of time where it’s not safe for our kids to be outdoors.

ROTT: But she also says this is part of our new normal, which is what I also heard from Zeke.

HAUSFATHER: Like, we’re not going to be able to put the fire genie back in the bottle and return to the climate we had in the 1980s, at least not anytime in the next few centuries. So, it’s not a good news story.

BARBER: Geez.

HAUSFATHER: But I also don’t want to let people off the hook, you know. We need to adapt–

BARBER: Wow.

HAUSFATHER: –to this world of more and more extreme fires.

ROTT: You know, but both Zeke and Lisa emphasize the thing that people can do is reduce their emissions, right, which is going to stop the problem from getting even worse. You know, so that means things like switching to renewable energy, regenerative farming practices, and, you know, making more urban green space to cool down cities. You know, but sadly, we all should settle into this new normal, too.

BARBER: And more inside time, I guess.

ROTT: Yeah.

[MUSIC PLAYING]

BARBER: Nate, thank you so much for bringing us this reporting.

ROTT: Yeah, happy to do it.

About this episode:   New research has identified massive fungal blooms in North America in the Cretaceous period, suggesting significant ecological upheaval before and after the asteroid wiped out the dinosaurs. In this episode: Arturo Casadevall, senior author of the study, explains why these findings are so significant and his hypothesis that fungi gave mammals an evolutionary leg-up over reptiles. Guest:  Dr. Arturo Casadevall, PhD, MS, is chair of the department of Molecular Microbiology and Immunology at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health and a global expert in host defense mechanisms, fungi, and antibody-based therapies. He is also co-author of the book What If Fungi Win? with Stephanie Desmon. Host:  Stephanie Desmon, MA, is a former journalist, author, and the director of public relations and communications for the Johns Hopkins Center for Communication Programs. As the climate changes, is the deadly flooding in Nepal a sign of things to come?

Duration: 00:06:01

Released: 2026

Series: Consider This from NPR

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

The flooding in Nepal has left hundreds dead and many more missing. Many scientists believe the flooding is the result of a melting glacier.What do we know about the link between the disaster in Nepal and climate change?This episode was produced by Henry Larson, Megan Lim and Kai McNamee, with audio engineering by Tiffany Vera Castro. Our director is Jonas Adams.It was edited by Sadie Babits, Sarah Handel and Tinbete Ermyas.Our interim executive producer is Courtney Dorning.Support public media with NPR+ and enjoy perks for over 25 podcasts like this one. Learn more at plus.npr.org.See pcm.adswizz.com for information about our collection and use of personal data for sponsorship and to manage your podcast sponsorship preferences.NPR Privacy Policy

Transcript

AILSA CHANG, HOST:

It’s CONSIDER THIS, where every day we go deep on one big news story. Today, the devastating flooding in Nepal and its links to climate change. Hundreds are dead, and many more are missing after catastrophic flash flooding along the Tibet-Nepal border.

ANDY THAPA: Nothing is visible - no houses, no roads, no any greenery in those areas and no blue water. Now it’s muddy water now.

CHANG: Andy Thapa is one of the rescuers trying to get to as many survivors as he can. He’s a charter and rescue pilot for a helicopter company in Nepal, and in 20 or so flights over the last two days, he shot some video and shared that video with us.

THAPA: We saw many people stuck in the middle of the mud. Half of the body were inside the mud and they were just seeking for help. But there were no place to land, even no place to drop for the rescue.

CHANG: Thapa said he was able to find survivors in some accessible parts of the mountainous region, but many were in bad shape.

THAPA: Personally, for me, I evacuated more than 120 people. They are muddy, like in their face, in their legs, might be body scratched, hand broken, leg broken and they terrified, you know, unable to speak.

CHANG: He told us about one man who was able to talk with him.

THAPA: He had 10 people in the family, out of which he lost nine in front of his eye.

CHANG: Thapa has been flying the most critically injured people to Nepal’s capital, Kathmandu, and the rest to a closer staging area. But there are so many people still stranded. Today, he said he had to leave several hundred people sheltering in a tunnel because the weather just got too bad to fly.

THAPA: But they are very safe and they have enough food to eat. We will just continue with such missions.

CHANG: CONSIDER THIS - climate change is accelerating the frequency of deadly natural disasters. What do we know about the role climate change is playing in the Himalayan region?

(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

CHANG: From NPR, I’m Ailsa Chang.

(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

CHANG: It’s CONSIDER THIS FROM NPR. These deadly flash floods on the border of Nepal and Chinese-administered Tibet are not a surprise to many climate scientists. NPR’s Julia Simon joins us now to explain why. Hi, Julia.

JULIA SIMON, BYLINE: Hello, Ailsa.

CHANG: So the way I understand it, this flooding - it came from a glacier - right? - that collapsed. Is that correct?

SIMON: Yeah. So the reports are still preliminary, but many scientists think this was a glacier collapsing - this giant chunk of ice that snapped off, triggering the floods. This mass of ice and rock and mud and water - it started ripping down this Himalayan river valley, down through these villages, destroying communities. If you’ve seen the videos, you can see it’s this avalanche of deadly debris.

CHANG: Yeah. And I mean, these glaciers, they’ve been around for thousands of years, right? So…

SIMON: Right.

CHANG: …To have a chunk of glacier just snap off now seems like a huge deal.

SIMON: It does. But sadly, many scientists are not so surprised that this could happen, Ailsa, and that’s because of human-caused climate change. These glaciers that were once stable - they’re disappearing. NPR has been reporting on Nepal’s glaciers for years. A team from NPR traveled to Nepal in 2022. They were climbing up a moraine. That’s a massive pile of boulders created by a glacier. But when NPR’s Rebecca Hersher got to the top, there was no glacier in sight.

(SOUNDBITE OF ARCHIVED NPR CONTENT)

REBECCA HERSHER: Oh, wow.

(SOUNDBITE OF WATER SPLASHING)

SIMON: That’s the sound of water. There was only water - this huge lake - where a glacier should have been, Ailsa. The glacier had melted, and glacial lake floods are a growing threat. In recent years, multiple glacial lake floods have displaced and killed people. Scientists warn that an estimated 15 million people around the world are at risk from such floods.

CHANG: Wow. So just how unstable are these melting glaciers?

SIMON: Yeah. So NPR spoke with Alton Byers about this. He’s at the Institute of Arctic and Alpine Research at the University of Colorado at Boulder. He says he’s surprised how fast the permafrost - that’s the continuously frozen ice, soil and rock that’s held the mountains together for millennia - it’s now changing.

ALTON BYERS: The analogy I and my colleagues use is take a stick of butter out of the refrigerator and put it on the counter. It’s going to get softer and it’s going to lose its structural integrity.

SIMON: Yeah. So these melting glaciers - they pose deadly risks to the communities in their path.

CHANG: I mean, we’re seeing it right now. So…

SIMON: Yeah.

CHANG: …What are some solutions here to prevent the kind of disaster that we are seeing in Nepal, for example, a disaster that is caused by melting glaciers?

SIMON: Well, the first thing is better alert systems. It seems right now, like, Nepal’s flood warning system wasn’t equipped to handle this, despite quite a bit of investment in those warning systems over the last decade or so. And it’s important to mention this area has seen flash floods before. But we also know the primary driver of global warming is the burning of fossil fuels, like oil, gas and coal. The solutions to transition away from fossil fuels are out there, Ailsa. They are scalable. They’re affordable. They’re reliable. They include solar energy, wind energy and batteries.

CHANG: That is NPR climate and energy correspondent Julia Simon. Thank you, Julia.

SIMON: Thank you, Ailsa.

Black Gold - The Future of Food We Throw Away

Black Gold: The Future of Food…We Throw Away

Duration: 00:51:11

Released: 2022

Series: Gastropod

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

For a few weeks in 1987, trash was temporarily headline news: a barge filled with waste that would no longer fit in New York City’s overflowing landfills spent months wandering up and down the East Coast with nowhere to dump its smelly, rotting cargo. The trash barge’s travels triggered a long overdue public rethink of the wisdom of sending all of our waste to landfills—including food. But fast forward more than thirty years, and food still takes up more space in American landfills than anything else. About 30 to 40 percent of food produced in the US gets thrown away, rather than eaten. What’s more, putting all that rotting food inside landfills produces a lot of methane, a powerful greenhouse gas. Our ancestors knew exactly what to do with food waste; the earliest descriptions of composting were written on clay tablets more than 4,000 years ago. So why didn’t the GarBarge kick off a composting craze? And why is it so hard for us to keep food waste out of landfills? This episode, Gastropod visits the future of food waste: the high-tech facilities as well as the innovative policies that promise to keep our discarded food out of landfills, keep methane from escaping into the atmosphere, and turn those food scraps into something useful. Can a state the size of California really keep 75 percent of its food waste out of landfills, as it has pledged to do by 2025—and what will happen if it does? Listen in for compost blow-dryers, fruit-sticker bingo, and a lot of microbes! Catching Fire

Duration: 00:54:43

Released: 2024

Series: Big Picture Science

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

We have too much “bad fire.” Not only destructive wildfires, but the combustion that powers our automobiles and provides our electricity has generated a worrying rise in atmospheric carbon dioxide. And that is driving climate change which is adding to the frequency of megafires. Now we’re seeing those effects in “fire-clouds,” pyrocumulonimbus events. But there’s such a thing as “good fire.” Indigenous peoples managed the land with controlled fires, reaped the benefits of doing so, and they’re bringing them back. So after millions of years of controlling fire, is it time for us to revisit our attitudes and policies, not just with regard to combustion, but how we manage our wildfires? Guests: David Peterson - Meteorologist, U.S. Naval Research Laboratory Stephen Pyne - Emeritus professor at Arizona State University, fire historian, urban farmer, author of “The Pyrocene: How We Created an Age of Fire, and What Happens Next” Richard Wrangham - Ruth B. Moore Research Professor of Biological Anthropology at Harvard University and author of “Catching Fire: How Coooking Made Us Human” Margo Robbins - Co-founder and president of the Cultural Fire Management Council (CFMC), organizer of the Cultural Burn Training Exchange (TREX) that takes place on the Yurok Reservation twice a year, and an enrolled member of the Yurok Tribe Originally aired May 9, 2022 Featuring music by Dewey Dellay and Jun Miyake Big Picture Science is part of the Airwave Media podcast network. Please contact advertising@airwavemedia.com to inquire about advertising on Big Picture Science.   • Speaker: 300:18:24

    Yeah. So heat exhaustion is, maybe I could call it a little bit of a less extreme version of heat related illness. It’s where people start to feel tired. Your body is no longer able to sweat, which is one of the primary ways that we kind of cool off when it’s very hot outside. By comparison, heat stroke looks very similar to a regular stroke. It’s dizziness. It’s, kind of loss of speech patterns. It’s, you know, changes in your ability to speak. It’s changes in your ability in your cognitive function. And it is generally something that requires more severe treatment. So, if you have heat exhaustion, you can generally treat that at home with hydration. One of the things that the mayor was mentioning earlier is that water is life. That’s very important. If you’re having a heat stroke, that should be you know, you should be looking out for some of those more severe symptoms, and that should be a good reason to visit an emergency room or an urgent care facility to get your core body temperature cooled down.

  • Speaker: 000:19:32

    Jeva, there are as many as twelve thousand recorded heat deaths in The US each year. How does that how do we account for that? How do we know that people have died because of extreme heat?

  • Speaker: 400:19:42

    It’s a complicated question. There’s about three different ways you can calculate excess or, heat deaths. One is direct and indirect. These are two different categories of looking at deaths. So direct deaths from heat, there’s a diagnostic code. It’s x 30. It’s a medical classification for a death due to excessive natural heat. This is distinct from excessive unnatural heat, such as a tanning bed death, a little bit of morbid trivia through the morning. But the direct deaths are exactly what we’re talking about in terms of heat exhaustion or heat stroke. The the classic what you think of as somebody dying from heat. Then there are excess dust or indirect dust from heat. That’s cardiovascular events, stroke, complications from diabetes, respiratory illness, all the other ways that heat can kill you that’s maybe a little bit more invisible. So if somebody comes into a hospital with a heart attack, the physicians are treating that heart attack. They’re not sitting there being like, is this heart attack from the heat outside right now? So it might not be coded as a direct heat death, but it’s still somebody who died from the heat. And there’s two different ways you can count these excess heat deaths. So one is to look at how many more people died compared to what you would have expected for that period of time. And then for so for an example of this is, in France, there were, twenty one thousand six hundred all cause deaths between June 17 and July 2. That was their heat wave in Europe. Statistical modeling would have expected about fifteen thousand nine hundred and ten deaths based on the previous year, so that gives us an excess of about seven or, five thousand seven hundred. And then the third way of doing this is basically a predictive model. So saying we know when the temperature rises by x amount, this number of people will die and kind of using that model to fit with, the temperatures that were recorded. So there’s a bunch of different ways to record excess deaths, a bunch of different models that need to be peer reviewed. And so this this work is still ongoing for heat events, both in Europe and in North America. But this is why you can see many different numbers for how many people died in a given heat wave.

  • Speaker: 000:21:50

  • Speaker: 300:22:40

    Yeah. That’s a great question. And I think that there are potentially two things that are happening here. And so I think people have definitely suffered from that long lack of exposure to the outdoors and to other people. And I think that has some, immunological implications for the long term. I think the other thing is that people who have experienced COVID illness, and at this point, we’ve gone through multiple waves of COVID, that might also have an individual level impact, as this person mentioned in their note. So people who have experienced maybe some of the symptoms of long COVID, we do talk about often how one of the most vulnerable groups, to extreme heat is people with preexisting medical conditions. So we do know that people who have underlying, for example, cardiovascular diseases or illnesses, people who have respiratory illnesses, people who have pre existing mental health conditions may be particularly susceptible when it’s very hot outside. And I would say the same is likely true for people who are experiencing the symptoms of long COVID. There might be some individual level vulnerability because of the long term medical impacts that you’re facing after having a period of COVID infection as well.

  • Speaker: 000:24:16

    I wanted to, Jeeva, talk to you about wildfire smoke. This year, along with the heat, we’ve seen wildfire smoke from Canada blanketing the Midwest and East Coast affecting a hundred and fifteen million Americans. What’s the relationship between extreme heat and wildfires? What’s made this year’s wildfire smoke especially bad?

  • Speaker: 400:24:35

    Yeah. So there’s, it’s there there’s a lot of relationships between wildfire smoke and heat. In particular, this year, the the smoke was directed over the most populated areas of The United States by the heat dome, which was raising all of those temperatures. So the temperatures got really hot in, Northern Minnesota and Ontario. There were lightning fires, and, that smoke then rotated around this heat dome that was making extreme temperatures, and blew the smoke into some of the most populated areas of the country. And this is, when we talk about, like, compounding climate crises, this is kind of the classic example of you wanna run your air conditioning, you wanna have your windows open because it’s so hot out, but at the same time, you need to protect yourself from the wildfire smoke. So these two crises really go hand in hand and make a kind of a perfect storm of, like, terrible conditions that you want to avoid.

  • Speaker: 000:25:34

    Yeah. Emerta, a study published in February by the journal Scientific Advance shows that wildfire smoke inhalation caused twenty four thousand deaths each year from 2006 to 2020. What effect does wildfire smoke have on our bodies?

  • Speaker: 300:25:53

    Well, we know that there are a lot of impacts from just overall air pollution exposure. There’s a pretty direct relationship between how much particulate matter you’re inhaling, especially the finer particulate matter that’s smaller in size, which can descend deeper into the lungs and cross the blood brain barrier and lead to a whole host of different health outcomes. And we know that that happens even at very low levels. So once we get into particulate matter that’s much of much higher in concentration, such as the, PM that we see from wildfires, we can see a lot of different health impacts from that. We see respiratory illnesses. We see cardiovascular illnesses. We see increases in stroke. There are a lot of different health impacts from, exposure to that high level of particulate matter. The other thing that I think is really important is, you know, in addition to people not being used to those very high levels, you know, in The United States, because PM 2.5 has historically since the Clean Air Act, been pretty heavily regulated, we’ve seen, pretty low concentrations on a regular basis of particulate matter. But when we have a wildfire where trees are burning or maybe urban areas are burning, we’ll see these huge spikes in the levels of PM 2.5, and people just aren’t used to it. People are experiencing these extreme levels, that that they haven’t been experiencing historically at baseline.

  • Speaker: 000:27:18

    Jeva, in a headline this month, heat map calls wildfire smoke the East Coast’s new forever problem. Are there federal guidelines that exist around wildfire smoke the way we’ve been talking about cities trying to deal with extreme heat? From a policy standpoint, are we doing enough to deal with this persistent problem?

  • Speaker: 400:27:38

    I don’t think so. This is such a new problem in parts of the country that there aren’t really standards for dealing with it. I think a really good example of this actually is that heat and air quality alerts are not typically considered together. These are two separate metrics and two separate alert systems. And, really, they should not be triggered independently. They should be considered together. Again, as you were talking about, they go hand in hand. And I think we’re just seeing the way that cities are kind of on their back foot with dealing with wildfire smoke when it comes to everything from deciding at what point we should cancel sporting events or outdoor events. There’s not really an AQI threshold that people talk about yet. And I think, yeah, it’s just an area that there’s a lot of opportunity to make decisions now before it gets worse.

  • Speaker: 300:39:13

    It’s a good question. And I think, you know, carrying water on their person so that they can continue to take sips of water throughout the day, it’s really important to stay hydrated. That’s the only way that we’re going to see, you know, less of these extreme health outcomes. And one of the things that I would argue from a public health perspective is that, yes, it could be disruptive to have a ten minute water break, but it would also be disruptive to have a worker who experiences heat stress or heat exhaustion or heat stroke and has to have a more severe medical intervention as a result. So I think it’s important for businesses to take those different trade offs into consideration. Do I give my workers a ten minute water breaks in order to ensure that I’m not they’re not experiencing a larger health event down the line? So, you know, I think I think it’s really important to make sure that people can have access to hydration, access to breaks, access to shaded spaces if they’re working outdoors.

  • Speaker: 300:41:02

    Yeah. We know that heat has really severe impacts on people’s physical health and what’s starting to become more apparent is that people’s mental health is really impacted as well. We’ve seen in some recent work that my team has done looking at emergency department visits associated with all different types of mental health causes, increases in emergency department visits as summertime temperatures increase. So this is for things like substance use disorders, schizophrenia, self harm, mood disorders, anxiety, stress disorders. There are a whole host of different mental illnesses that present and get exacerbated

  • Speaker: 000:41:36

    during periods of extreme heat. We got this email from Jordan. Amrita, I wonder if you can just answer it quickly. Jordan says, I’ve heard that those who take SSRI medications for anxiety or depression have a more difficult time regulating body temperature and wind up suffering a bit more in excessive heat. Is there any truth to this?

  • Speaker: 300:41:55

    Yes. This is actually, a documented pathway. SSRIs can cause heat intolerance because they interfere with the hypothalamus, which is the temperature regulator in the brain. This also, can increase the risk of things like dehydration, heat exhaustion, and heat stroke. I think SSRIs also have been shown to, interfere with sweat production as well. So, you know, taking SSRIs can impair your body’s cooling ability. They can also disrupt the thirst mechanism. So they can make it harder to know when your body is actually getting thirsty, which is a signal that starts the pathway of dehydration. So again, all of the same things that we’ve been talking about previously, just making sure that you’re staying hydrated, important for folks who have underlying medical conditions that require treatment with SSRIs.

  • Speaker: 000:42:45

    Jibo, I just wanna shift the conversation back to the idea of federal policy, federal funding. The Trump administration is proposing a $1,700,000,000 or about 27% cut to NOAA as part of the 2027 fiscal splint spending plan. How will these cuts affect forecasting, if at all?

  • Speaker: 400:43:04

    Yeah. This is a really important point, and there’s a lot of things that worry me about this, but the most relevant to our conversation is that staff cuts would mean things like weather balloon launches and sensor maintenance are no longer happening. Weather balloon launches, happen regularly. They help us measure wind, temperature, humidity, pressure, and make those severe weather forecasts. And the administration has said that they want to pivot to AI, that they think that this is a way forward in forecasting, but artificial intelligence is only as strong as the data that’s being input. And if we’re not getting that robust data from the field, which takes takes boots on the ground, takes people out there launching the balloons themselves or fixing sensors at sea, then we’re just not the quality of our forecast is going to be severely degraded.

  • Speaker: 000:43:51

I think there are a lot of other resources that are available, that we’ve been talking about over the course of the last little bit. Climate Change and Extreme Heat End of Summer Episode with Dr. Kelly Turner and Dr. Ladd Keith

Duration: 00:53:01

Released: 2023

Series: America Adapts - The Climate Change Podcast

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

In episode 192 of America Adapts, Doug Parsons hosts Dr. Kelly Turner and Dr. Ladd Keith. Kelly is an Associate Professor of Urban Planning at UCLA and Ladd is an assistant professor in the School of Landscape Architecture and Planning at the University of Arizona.  We learn how extreme heat disproportionately impacts marginalized communities.  We also explore the evolving media coverage of extreme heat. The three of us break down the highs and lows of all the media coverage of extreme heat this past summer.  We also learn about Kelly’s research on heat’s impact on California schools, and what that means for both public health and student learning.  We take a dive into federal approaches to heat management and learn about the glaring disparity in resources and attention allocated to extreme heat compared to other climate hazards and ponder the potential for FEMA to classify extreme heat as a disaster declaration.  We also discuss the value of having a national adaptation plan for extreme heat planning. Finally, and yes, we go there, we get an update on the efforts to name heat waves. We highlight the pros and cons of this approach and discuss other options.  It is a gangbuster episode and that’s just a sampling of what we cover. Transcripts for this episode available here. (These are computer generated so pardon the errors.) Topics Covered:  Dr. Turner specializes in heat equity research, while Dr. Keith focuses on heat policy planning and governance. Increasing prevalence of extreme heat due to climate change and its disproportionate impact on marginalized groups. Media coverage of extreme heat is becoming more sophisticated, highlighting impacts on workers and public health. Dr. Turner’s research in California focuses on schools and heat’s impact on student learning. Discussion of heating planning strategies including shade in urban design. The Biden administration is updating heat safety labor laws, emphasizing the need for comprehensive regulations at state and federal levels and the potential for FEMA labeling heat events as disaster declarations. Naming heat waves to increase public awareness is debated, highlighting the importance of reaching vulnerable populations. Will heat lead to managed retreat from regions that become uninhabitable? A national adaptation plan is emphasized to address heat impacts, especially for marginalized communities. Designing for climate change

Duration: 00:24:30

Released: 2018

Series: Better Off

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

A new study from Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health found that students who lived in dormitories without air conditioning during a heat wave performed worse on a series of simple tests compared with students who lived in air-conditioned dorms. The findings show that the effects of extreme heat are not just felt by those typically thought of as vulnerable—such as the elderly. And with global temperatures on the rise, the research underscores the need for sustainable design solutions in mitigating the health impacts of extreme heat. In this week’s episode, we’ll speak with the authors of that study to learn how we can  better design our buildings and cities to adapt to our changing climate. You’ll hear from Joe Allen, assistant professor of exposure assessment science and director of the Healthy Buildings Program at the Center for Climate, Health, and the Global Environment, and Jose Guillermo (Memo) Cedeño Laurent, associate director of the Healthy Buildings Program.

Does It Feel Like Mosquitoes Are Getting Worse?

Duration: 00:13:19

Released: 2025

Series: Short Wave

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

Mosquitoes are the deadliest animal on the planet, and some of them may be on the rise. At least in listener Abigail Krich-Starr’s area, that’s due to warmer, wetter weather — which, yes, is linked to climate change. But it doesn’t stop there: Ecologists and entomologists say increased heat could also alter mosquito behavior, shift their natural habitat, and even change how pathogens incubate and spread inside their bodies.So how do you protect yourself against the (mosquito) masses? Our experts suggested several things:- Assess your risk by checking local mosquito surveillance efforts, like this one for the state of Massachusetts- Consider rescheduling outdoor events happening between dusk and dawn, which is peak biting time for multiple mosquito species- Wear long-sleeved shirts and pants, weather-permitting, to limit exposed skin- Use an EPA-approved DEET repellent, and/or a permethrin spray for clothing and outdoor gearThis episode is part of Nature Quest, a monthly Short Wave segment that answers listener questions about the local environment.Got a question about changes in your local environment? Send a voice memo to shortwave@npr.org with your name, where you live and your question. We might make it into our next Nature Quest episode! Fire And Ice - Linking Intense Wildfire And The Melting Arctic

Duration: 00:12:13

Released: 2023

Series: Short Wave

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

In the Arctic Ocean, sea ice is shrinking as the climate heats up. In the Western U.S., wildfires are getting increasingly destructive. Those two phenomena are thousands of miles apart, but scientists are uncovering a surprising connection. The ice is connected to weather patterns that reach far across North America. And as the climate keeps changing and sea ice shrinks, Western states could be seeing more extreme weather, the kind that fuels extreme wildfires.Check out the full series about how melting ice affects us all: npr.org/icemelt. Growing a Healthier Climate

Duration: 00:17:23

Released: 2026

Series: Humans in Public Health

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

In this episode, we sit down with Professor Meredith Niles to explore how climate-friendly agriculture—from methane-reducing rice irrigation to no-till farming—is actually a quiet powerhouse for public health. While traditional environmental messaging has hit a wall, shifting the narrative to human health benefits (like keeping heavy metals out of baby food and clearing our air) might be the key to finally getting farmers, consumers and policymakers on board. It’s time to take off the carbon goggles and look at the health potential of sustainable farming.
