Transcript

0:02 · This isn’t a forest.

0:04 · Not really.

0:05 · It’s actually an urban park in the densely populated city of Nanchang, China.

0:11 · Filled with birds, native trees…

0:14 · and volcanic rock on 137 acres of urban land.

0:19 · It’s called a sponge city.

0:21 · And it’s a type of natural infrastructure that architects around the world are designing to help us combat and withstand a changing climate.

0:29 · So what does it mean for a city to become spongy?

0:38 · This video is presented by Delta Airlines.

0:42 · Today, like everywhere else on the globe cities are feeling the effects of climate change.

0:46 · And they often don’t have the right infrastructure to help them survive its impacts.

0:52 · Some neighborhoods in Houston don’t have enough trees to provide shade and relief from rising temperatures.

0:58 · Meanwhile, rising sea levels threaten coastal cities, like Mumbai and Seoul that don’t often have the infrastructure to handle more frequent flooding.

1:06 · This is only made worse by the fact that cities are covered with pavement and other surfaces that can’t absorb water.

1:13 · Urbanization changes habitat.

1:15 · So, when you build on a place it removes vegetation and alters hydrology.

1:22 · That’s Dr. Charlie Nilon.

1:24 · He told me that cities play a complicated role in the climate crisis…

1:28 · especially when it comes to maintaining biodiversity.

1:31 · Biodiversity is, simply put the total number of plants and animals that occur someplace.

1:39 · Biodiversity is one of our main support systems for human life on earth.

1:44 · We rely on natural ecosystems full of diverse plants, animals, and organisms for essential things like clean water, food, and medicine.

1:54 · And biodiversity is critical to defending the Earth against climate change.

1:59 · We need healthy forests to absorb carbon dioxide from the air and enough native plants for pollinators to help crops flourish.

2:07 · But studies show that as urban expansion continues it can drive habitat losses that directly put some species in danger…

2:13 · profoundly impacting global biodiversity.

2:16 · Cities have a big impact on what happens locally.

2:19 · Cities capture a significant number of plant and animal species…

2:23 · which are native.

2:24 · So that, even in cities, there’s a significant amount of biodiversity.

2:28 · Which means they can play a role in saving these local plant and animal species.

2:33 · The really important thing to think about is not so much how urbanization…

2:38 · reduces biodiversity, but really how in cities you can conserve biodiversity.

2:43 · One way to do this is by creating environments where nature can flourish.

2:47 · Environments that can also help cities soak up excess water.

2:52 · And that’s where sponge cities come in.

2:55 · In 2015, China launched a sponge city pilot program in 30 cities across the country.

3:01 · The goal of the program was to coordinate and promote the construction of sponge cities…

3:06 · which would improve urban drainage and flood prevention and create a diverse biological environment.

3:13 · Architects did this by supplementing the existing grey infrastructure that relied on concrete pipes and dams with natural solutions like gardens that are designed to capture rain and native trees that suck up excess water through their roots.

3:27 · In China, these ideas were taken from ancient drainage systems.

3:31 · That’s because for centuries Chinese cities actually handled water pretty well in part because they were built with nature in mind.

3:39 · A study from the scientific journal Water recreated some of these old practices.

3:44 · Building roofs designed with sloped eaves allowed rainwater to fall onto the permeable pavement below…

3:50 · and exterior walls were lined with flowers and trees to absorb rain and stormwater.

3:55 · Today, natural solutions like these have been reimplemented in major cities across China.

4:04 · In Qian’an, a sewage pipe was replaced with this natural infiltration system…

4:09 · that uses vegetation beds to purify storm and rainwater.

4:13 · Shanghai replaced concrete roads and sidewalks with this permeable pavement which is lined with vegetation to absorb excess water into the soil underground.

4:23 · And across Wuhan, dozens of urban gardens like these were planted to absorb water before it overwhelms communities.

4:31 · Most of these projects rely on introducing vegetation in an urban area to soak up excess water.

4:37 · This is a really great way to handle flooding and it also supports urban biodiversity.

4:43 · Because you’re providing habitat for additional groups…

4:46 · Plant and animal species.

4:47 · Sometimes you can restore habitats that have been lost.

4:50 · Despite these benefits, sponge cities can’t fix everything.

4:54 · The connection between climate change and flooding is still being studied.

4:58 · But when floods do happen these spongy cities are only able to absorb excess water up to a certain point.

5:05 · In 2021, sponge city designs in Zhengzhou failed when historic rainfall drenched the city.

5:11 · More than 300 people died from the catastrophic flooding.

5:15 · There are other limitations, too.

5:17 · One review of sponge cities in Guian New District found that national standards and codes for spongy infrastructure are really difficult to enforce because climate, hydrology, and even socio-economics are vastly different from one city to the next.

5:33 · So what might work in some cities won’t necessarily solve climate-related problems in others.

5:39 · Plus, ambitious sponge city designs like the sprawling parks built in China are expensive and require a lot of space…

5:45 · which most cities don’t have.

5:47 · So some US cities experimenting with natural infrastructure like LA and Boston are using the green space they already have.

5:56 · By trying things like planting vegetation on curbs…

5:59 · and creating green spaces along median strips.

6:03 · One type of city design certainly won’t save us from climate change…

6:07 · but they can make a difference in how we live with it.

6:10 · We often talk about biodiversity like it’s far away, but it’s actually right there.

6:16 · And I think the more that people understand that the kinds of things they do around where they live that really matters.

6:21 · People can do things to change cities.

6:24 · They can do things to make cities different.

6:27 · They can do things to make cities better.

How melting glaciers contributed to Himalayas flooding

Episode of (Season ) - Released: - Duration: 00:08:45

Description

How melting glaciers contributed to the flooding disaster in the Himalayas

Transcript

AMNA NAWAZ: Welcome to the “News Hour.”

The scale of the disaster in the Himalayas is becoming clearer tonight after catastrophic flooding tore through parts of Nepal and neighboring Tibet.

Scientists now believe the disaster was likely triggered by the collapse of a glacier high in the mountains, unleashing a torrent of water, mud, and debris that swept away bridges, roads, and entire communities.

GEOFF BENNETT: President Trump today called the devastation in Nepal brutal.

The death toll stands at least 390, with more than 1,400 others still missing.

That’s as rescue crews race to reach survivors in some of the world’s most difficult terrain.

In the valleys that carve through the highest peaks in the world, villages are now buried in mud.

This is Nepal’s Nuwakot district.

Yesterday’s floods left behind a layer of sludge so high, it reached rooftops and trapped trucks as if in quicksand.

Survivors recounted the terror as it all unfolded without warning.

JANAK BAHADUR, Flood Survivor (through translator): It came in suddenly and exploded like a bomb.

The river looked small from a distance, but it suddenly started overflowing and swept through this place.

GEOFF BENNETT: Geological experts believe a glacier collapse some 30 miles north of Kathmandu was likely what caused the disaster.

These satellite images show the glacier along those snowy peaks there at the bottom before the collapse.

And this is what it looks like now, the surrounding rivers swollen by the floodwaters and debris rushing downstream.

Cell phone video showed the torrent starting to barrel toward this hydroelectric dam.

Within seconds, it bursts through the other side and engulfs the entire site, this border crossing between Nepal and Tibet, the autonomous region in China, now completely destroyed by the ensuing landslides.

Nepalese officials say the floods have swept away dozens of bridges and damaged some 25 miles of roads.

That, combined with the already treacherous terrain of the Himalayas, have made rescue efforts especially difficult.

Nepal’s army says it’s airlifted more than 100 people to safety using helicopters.

On the ground, crews trudge through knee-deep mud with the aid of search dogs to locate the missing, hoisting them onto their backs, many survivors transported to hospitals in Kathmandu.

But as the search grinds on, many families have no choice but to wait for news they fear may never come.

SUBINA TAMANG, Wife of Missing Man (through translator): All I want is that my husband comes back to me safely in one piece.

I do not want anything else.

CHAMINDI SHRESHTHA, Mother of Missing Man (through translator): My son is stuck in the floods.

He is a truck driver.

And he said he was driving.

There is no news of him.

We are not able to find out anything.

GEOFF BENNETT: The scale of this catastrophe is being felt across the region.

Officials in China say they’re searching for hundreds of people still missing in Tibet, where floodwaters stretched for miles across the vast plateau.

Authorities said a new lake was formed in the deluge.

A spokesperson for China’s Foreign Ministry vowed to send aid to Nepal, which has significantly fewer emergency resources than its neighbor.

LIN JIAN, Chinese Foreign Ministry Spokesperson (through translator): The mudslide has caused massive casualties and left many missing in both China and Nepal.

The two sides are having close communication and coordinating efforts for search-and-rescue.

GEOFF BENNETT: Among the missing, some 500 foreign nationals.

The U.S.

State Department this morning confirmed three Americans had been rescued, but as many as 90 may have been affected by the floods.

SADHGURU JAGGI VASUDEV, Indian Spiritual Leader (through translator): I wish to bring to your notice that there has been a terrible… GEOFF BENNETT: In an emotional message today, the Indian spiritual leader known as Sadhguru said a group had been on a holy pilgrimage in the area and got caught in the floods.

He said 22 of them were U.S.

citizens.

And for more on what caused the devastating floods, we’re joined now by Dan Shugar, who heads the waterSHED Lab at the University of Calgary.

Thanks for being with us.

DAN SHUGAR, waterSHED Lab Director, University of Calgary: Thank you.

GEOFF BENNETT: So scientists believe the deadly flood in Nepal may have started when a massive chunk of ice snapped off a glacier high in the Himalayas, unleashing this devastating cascade into the valley below.

Walk us through how that might have happened.

DAN SHUGAR: That was certainly our initial thought as to what happened based on the information that we had yesterday from some preliminary satellite information.

It’s changed a little bit since then, but the general story is still the same.

At about just under 5,000 meters elevation is a glacier that in the satellite imagery that we saw yesterday essentially two days ago was there, yesterday was gone, and the flood resulted downstream.

Since then, we have found that glacier ice - - chunk of glacier ice that essentially disappeared downstream was part of a much larger mass of bedrock, so a gigantic chunk of the mountainside that collapsed, essentially exploding as it descended a vertical kilometer to the valley bottom, melting all that ice, and then flowing out and generating the floods that we saw in those horrific videos.

GEOFF BENNETT: What makes that part of the Himalayas so vulnerable?

DAN SHUGAR: That’s a good question.

I suppose it would be a couple of things, one, incredibly steep topography and then huge relief, so massive differences between the low points and the high points, lots of glaciers, lots of glacial lakes, which weren’t an issue in this particular disaster, but certainly in other ones.

A lot of seismic activity.

The Gorkha earthquake wasn’t that long ago, and we saw very similar sort of disasters unfold, in fact, just on the other side of this mountain.

And the human part of that equation, I think, is important.

Unlike in Western North America, in much of the Himalaya, you have settlements in these incredibly high, narrow valleys that essentially are putting people and infrastructure and livelihoods at risk when these landslides or these floods come down.

GEOFF BENNETT: The other human element at play, it strikes me, is climate change.

What role does climate change play here?

What can scientists say with confidence about the connection between rising temperatures, retreating glaciers, and disasters like this one?

DAN SHUGAR: We can speak in generalities, because it’s a little bit hard to speak about the specific event just yet because we haven’t done any sort of attribution study.

As temperatures rise, glaciers will melt, will thin vertically, destabilizing the valley walls that they formerly were sort of help – hoping – or helping to hold up.

Additionally, as air temperatures rise, the permafrost is changing, and so permafrost is just ground that remains frozen year-round year after year.

But in some areas, both at high altitudes, but also in the north, permafrost is destabilizing because of warming temperatures.

And that can cause large landslides like this as well.

And then a third factor, I think would be changes in precipitation patterns, not only in terms of intensity, but in terms of when during the year they occurred.

Water can get underneath the glaciers, allowing them to slide a little bit more rapidly, any number of ways that that extra water can sort of enhance or increase the likelihood of one of these kinds of events.

GEOFF BENNETT: Dan, what lessons can be learned from this disaster?

DAN SHUGAR: I think that these landscapes, these steep mountain landscapes, are continually evolving due to climate change.

And the global community, not just the government of Nepal or China or India, need – the global community, we need to be stepping up to help mitigate these risks and to enhance the resiliency of people living in these environments that are subject to these disasters that, when there’s a climate change link, we in the rich West are largely responsible for.

GEOFF BENNETT: Dan Shugar with the waterSHED Lab at the University of Calgary, thanks for joining us this evening.

DAN SHUGAR: Thank you.

Video (12:46)
Description

In Singapore, water supply impacted wars, the economy and people’s health. Now, one of the most water-stressed countries is teaching the world how to handle water when there is hardly any. 

We’re destroying our environment at an alarming rate. But it doesn’t need to be this way. Our new channel Planet A explores the shift towards an eco-friendly world — and challenges our ideas about what dealing with climate change means. We look at the big and the small: What we can do and how the system needs to change. Every Friday we’ll take a truly global look at how to get us out of this mess.

Transcript
Intro

0:01 · No joke, but this place…

0:02 · has a serious issue with water.

0:06 · Water here has changed the course of war, started fights with neighboring countries, and dominates the city’s political agenda.

0:14 · And yet, this country is a role model.

0:18 · “Singapore got it right, as simple as that.”

0:20 · This is how Singapore is transforming itself from one of the most water-stressed countries in the world to self-sufficiency, from water rags – to riches.

The challenge

0:36 · Welcome to Singapore!

0:37 · A vibrant multicultural city-state, right on the equator.

0:41 · Densely populated, around 6 million people live on this tiny island.

0:45 · It’s an international hub for trade and finance.

0:48 · It’s clean, safe, modern, wealthy, green.

0:52 · Basically, it has a lot of everything you’d want.

0:55 · There’s just that one thing…

0:56 · Singapore has no natural freshwater resources.

1:00 · And the way its economy and population are growing, total water demand could almost double by 2060.

1:06 · But Singapore is on it.

1:10 · “None of what they’ve done is magic.”

1:13 · That’s Peter Gleick, he’s an award-winning water expert.

1:16 · He puts Singapore’s success down to what he calls the “soft path” approach to their problems.

1:21 · “So the soft path for water is in contrast to what we have done over the last century and more, more than a century, the hard path.

1:29 · To build hard infrastructure, to take more and more water out of nature, to ignore the consequences of our water policies for nature, to build narrow institutions.

1:39 · And the soft path tries to turn that around and say, let’s use water efficiently and carefully.

1:45 · Let’s stop wasting water.

1:47 · Let’s look at new sources of supply.”

1:50 · To understand how Singapore can pull this off, you first need to understand where they’re coming from.

History

1:58 · “The Singapore water story starts with World War 2.”

2:02 · That’s Jon Church, the guy for water at the United Nations.

2:06 · Singaporeans would argue that the story goes back a lot longer, but 1942 is certainly when the issue grabbed the world’s attention.

2:13 · That’s when allied troops, namely British, Australian and Indian forces, were battling fascist Japan on Singapore Island, which at the time was a British colony.

2:24 · The allies depended on imported water which was a bit of a problem when Japanese forces blew up the pipes transporting that water.

2:30 · “The bridge that connects Malaysia and Singapore was bombed.

2:35 · The result was lack of water in the city, a terrible episode of water scarcity.”

2:43 · The Allies lost the battle of Singapore, but after Japan’s overall surrender, the island remained in British hands.

2:50 · It took more than 20 years until it became fully independent.

2:53 · But political liberation didn’t free Singapore from its water crisis: water rationing, catastrophic sanitation, and regular floods continued.

3:01 · With its fate now in its own hands, Singapore began planning for the long-term.

3:06 · Cecilia Tortajada has spent almost three decades researching water policy and innovations around the world and is fascinated by Singapore’s path.

3:15 · “When Singapore became independent, they started planning to be water independent, as well as food independent, energy independent, but to have systems that can sustain stress.

3:28 · That was in 1965 for 2060.”

Step 1: Imported Water

3:32 · Their initial masterplan was fine-tuned over the years into a water strategy which they call their “4 national taps”.

3:40 · First: Water imports Second: Desalination Third: Local Catchment And fourth, something they call “NEWater”.

3:50 · Let’s dive into it!

3:53 · Firstly, Singapore knew there was plenty of water, right here: Two deals to import water from Malaysia in the 60’s, got the taps running again.

4:03 · It’s cheap and millions of liters are pumped over the border every day.

4:07 · But buying half your water from your neighbor isn’t sustainable long-term.

4:12 · On the contrary: From the start, Malaysia made threats to cut off the supply and argued over its price.

4:18 · The tensions have even led to warnings of a military conflict.

4:21 · Singapore’s leaders know their weak spot, so they want to stop importing water by 2061.

4:27 · So, it’s all the more important that the other three taps become more than a drop in the ocean.

4:31 · “Water planning is very important because they have so little water, Singapore must make sure, that this water is used wisely.

4:41 · And the objective of this master plan is to make the most out of every single drop of water.”

4:48 · This means keeping rivers and drains clean, investing billions, starting to collect more water, cleaning it and use what the island state Singapore already has.

Step 2: Desalination

4:57 · For example, this: The ocean!

5:02 · “Desalination, Desalination, Desalination.

5:05 · You take that undrinkable salt water, you remove the salt and you have an unlimited supply of fresh water.”

5:11 · Do you remember?

5:12 · We had an entire video about obtaining fresh water through desalination.

5:16 · It’s used in a number of extremely dry places around the world and is a real gamechanger for some.

5:23 · “Membrane desalination uses pressure.

5:25 · Salt water, here coloured in red for clarity, is pressed through a membrane that is only partially permeable.

5:30 · Fresh water can press through.

5:32 · Here coloured in blue.

5:33 · But the salt is trapped on the other side.”

5:35 · Singapore really pushed to advance this technology.

5:38 · This underground facility for example is state-of-the-art.

5:42 · Normally, it treats used water.

5:44 · In times of drought, it desalinates sea water.

5:47 · On top, people use it as a park.

5:49 · Today, five desalination plants provide up to 25 % of the entire island’s water supply.

5:55 · Singapore plans increase this capacity to meet 30% of its needs in 2060, by which time water use is expected to have doubled.

6:04 · But that’s no way near self-sufficiency.

6:07 · So, what’s next?

Step 3: Local Catchment

6:09 · Bingo! Rain!

6:11 · A lot of it, and Singapore is a master of maxing that out.

6:15 · Two third of the entire state’s surface is used for rainwater catchment.

6:20 · “Basically, the water falls on the roof and then goes through drains and then you make sure that as little water leaks from the drains and is captured by the drainage system and conveyed to reservoirs where it can be stored.

6:43 · And treated for further use.

6:46 · An extensive network of rivers, canals and drains channels the water into 17 reservoirs.

6:51 · The biggest is Marina Barrage, with an area of 10,000 hectares.

6:55 · It holds back fresh water from flowing into the ocean.

6:58 · And helps to control frequent floods.

7:02 · So does this underground tank that catches floodwater in extreme cases when the drains spill over.

7:08 · Even this water could potentially be treated and reused.

7:11 · By 2060, the government wants to use 90% of the land area for rain catchment.

Step 4: NEWater

7:18 · Catching rainwater is one thing, but what about the water already in circulation?

7:25 · “All drainage water is collected, and treated and reused as much as possible.

7:32 · Most countries, they do not invest, not even a fraction of what Singapore is investing.”

7:38 · The water authorities built a 206 kilometer sewage pipe-highway, costing 10 billion dollars that guides the city’s sewage into state-of-the-art reclamation and cleaning facilities.

7:50 · Ok, Singapore is by some measures the 5th richest country in the world so it can afford to splash out like that.

7:56 · It’s also a lot easier to push through such projects when you have the sort of “controlled democracy”, run by the same party, that Singapore has had since independence in 1965.

8:07 · Anyway, the entire city’s underground is basically one big sewer network.

8:12 · But the pride of Singapore’s water strategy is what happens after collecting the water – the treatment.

8:19 · The authorities call it NEWater.

8:21 · It’s produced by microfiltration, reverse osmosis and UV radiation.

8:29 · “Some of that water is so high quality, Singapore is using it in their chip manufacturing industry, which requires ultra-pure water, which is an indication of how good a quality of that water is.”

8:42 · Did you know, that around the world, half of our wastewater is released untreated?

8:46 · And only 11% is actually reused.

8:49 · In Singapore about 30% percent of the entire drinkable and non-drinkable demand can be met by recycling used water.

8:57 · And they want to increase that to 55% by 2060.

9:02 · Most of it is used by industry, only a fraction of it is mixed into the drinking water supply, because we’re all so squeamish.

9:09 · “It’s controversial because we’re taking wastewater which, you know, goes down our drains, we flush it down our toilets, industry dumps wastewater.

9:17 · And we typically think of that as a, a liability, as something to get rid of.”

Policy and Education

9:22 · So how do you get people on board?

9:25 · “Using the flush saver cistern bag and save with every flush!”

9:29 · “Make saving water pay!”

9:30 · “Reduce water consumption!”

9:32 · “Save up to 10 liters of water per hour!”

9:35 · Nationwide campaigns encourage people to install water-saving fittings.

9:38 · And nicely, if you install those fittings, you get discount vouchers for other sustainable products.

9:44 · Digital water meters track each household’s water consumption and detect leaks in pipes.

9:49 · That’s how Singapore comes down to an incredible 5% water loss from leaks.

9:53 · Which is first class.

9:55 · Globally, the average is estimated to be 30%.

9:58 · “What they’ve done incredibly well, in my opinion, is on the side of water education.

10:04 · They’ve educated their community about their water situation, about their water challenges, and about the solutions that they’ve chosen to push.”

Conclusion

10:14 · As good as Singapore is doing, it has big advantages over other countries.

10:18 · Besides being rich and a tightly-controlled society, the city only has a tiny agricultural sector and can focus almost entirely on urban and industrial wastewater.

10:29 · So, they have one problem less as food production pollutes and consumes enormous amounts of fresh water in many parts of the world.

10:36 · Despite that, they’ve shown: a lot is possible.

10:41 · “Everywhere in the world, when we start, again talking about water - when we see our management, how can it be improved?

10:50 · Who would be involved?

10:52 · How can we pay for it?

10:54 · There is always a “but”.

10:55 · “But” can we do it?

10:56 · And in Singapore it’s not.

10:58 · In Singapore it’s: how is it that we are going to do it?

11:01 · So, it’s a very different perspective, its proactive.

11:06 · They move very fast.

11:08 · So once a decision has been taken the things start happening.”

11:14 · So, to sum up: Incentivizing low-cost water-saving fittings could be easy for every government to implement.

11:20 · Desalination is only an option for coastal regions and it requires a lot of energy, often dirty fossil fuels.

11:27 · Singapore’s catchment, treatment and purification facilities are world class, as the result of a lot of investment in innovation and research.

11:34 · “But it turns out that a dollar invested in an efficiency improvement is much more productive than a dollar invested in new supply.”

11:45 · Reusing wastewater seems a no-brainer.

11:47 · It’s safe and tested to work and the tech is available.

11:52 · “This is really important to find the best solutions to every country’s problems, but also long-term planning.

12:01 · Singapore, from all the way from independence has been planning its way through water security.

12:09 · In Singapore it’s extremely high on the political agenda, all the way up to the Prime Minister.

12:16 · It’s something which is mainstreamed in each and every policy adopted and implemented by the country.”

12:25 · Singapore is blazing a trail in tackling some of the world’s biggest water challenges in the face of climate change.

12:30 · It’s not just about money, but also about decision-making, policy and long-term planning.

12:35 · Some of the ingredients with which any region could make it from water rags to riches.

How Singapore Uses Science to Stay Cool

Bloomberg Originals, 03 17, 2021

Video (9:49)
Description

Heat waves kill more people than any other extreme weather event: more than tornados, hurricanes, and even floods. That’s why scientists are coming up with novel, new designs to help keep temperatures down in Singapore.

Like this video? Subscribe: http://www.youtube.com/Bloomberg?sub_confirmation=1

Become a Quicktake Member for exclusive perks: http://www.youtube.com/bloomberg/join

QuickTake Originals is Bloomberg’s official premium video channel. We bring you insights and analysis from business, science, and technology experts who are shaping our future. Subscribe for business news, but not as you’ve known it: exclusive interviews, fascinating profiles, data-driven analysis, and the latest in tech innovation from around the world.

Visit our partner channel QuickTake News for breaking global news and insight in an instant.

Transcript
Intro

0:01 · If you look at a thermal image of a city and then compare that to a map of vegetation, you’ll find that where there’s greenery, the temperature is lower.

0:11 · That’s because things like asphalt, concrete and shingled roofs absorb more heat from the sun than trees.

0:19 · This is the urban heat island effect, and it accounts for the higher temperatures in cities, often by several degrees compared with their surroundings.

0:28 · It’s becoming a huge risk to human health as growing urban populations exacerbate the heating effects of climate change.

0:36 · Heat waves kill more people than any other extreme weather event, more than tornadoes, hurricanes and even floods.

0:47 · That’s why urban heat island mitigation strategies are being studied in Singapore by a group of researchers.

0:54 · The government-backed project called Cooling Singapore is now in the process of combining everything they’ve learned to create a digital tool that can help cities all over the world, starting with Singapore.

Cooling Singapore

1:10 · In Singapore, close to the equator, temperatures regularly rise above 32 degrees Celsius or 90 degrees Fahrenheit.

1:19 · And the city structures only make it worse.

1:22 · And that is also the case for Singapore, which is basically a concrete jungle, more urbanized, more developed city.

1:29 · And even in Singapore, what you have is a situation whereby there’s a temperature difference of 7 degrees Celsius between the more urbanized and the more rural areas.

1:41 · The government has taken drastic steps to keep temperatures down.

1:46 · This is Gardens by the Bay, an award-winning park.

1:50 · And inside this greenhouse it’s a pleasant 24 degrees.

1:54 · That’s because the dome, along with two dozen nearby towers full of thousands of people, is chilled by what’s probably the world’s largest underground district cooling system.

2:04 · It uses a large central plant that cools water and then pipes it into banks, residential towers, an exhibition center, shopping malls and the city’s iconic Marina Bay Sands hotel and casino complex.

2:18 · So one of the biggest perks of using this system for the buildings is that they can save 40% in terms of electricity usage compared to your traditional air conditioners.

2:32 · And with Singapore relying on natural gas for most of its power, this new system means emissions savings equivalent to removing 10,000 cars from the city’s roads.

2:43 · That has big implications for the rest of the world.

2:45 · If things stay as they are, more than a third of the world’s electricity could end up being used to cool buildings and vehicles by 2050.

2:55 · As the world gets hotter, gets warmer, there is a greater need for air conditioning and as well as refrigerators, for instance.

3:03 · And the more people are buying these household appliances, the more energy usage they use and they release heat more, and that then exacerbates climate change.

3:18 · It’s a vicious circle.

Vegetation

3:20 · And so since 2017, researchers at Cooling Singapore have been identifying design solutions that reduce our need for so much cool air in the first place.

3:31 · One thing many cities have in common, and that’s the importance of vegetation.

3:36 · That’s a very important measure to mitigate the urban heat because of the shading effect, of course, and deep psychological effects of the vegetation.

3:46 · And also because of the possible evaporative cooling effect of the vegetation.

3:53 · Vegetation can be, of course, on the ground floor in form of trees and shrubs.

3:58 · And you can walk under them.

4:00 · This is the so-called canopy layer that the vegetation forms above us.

4:05 · But vegetation can also go up the facades of buildings and it can go to the roof of the buildings.

Garden City Feel

4:14 · Luckily, Singapore has been striving for the Garden City feel for quite some time.

4:20 · It was a vision initially introduced by then Prime Minister Lee Kuan Yew in 1967 to make life more pleasant for people.

4:28 · And today, Singapore is one of the world’s greenest cities in terms of urban vegetation.

4:33 · Kampung Admiralty, a community center that contains health facilities and social spaces, now provides more green space than the plot of land it was originally built on.

4:44 · It’s topped by a roofscape of staggered terraces covered in local plants, which functions as a community park, and a village green in the center that contains farm plots for residents to tend to.

4:56 · Parkroyal on Pickering was designed as a hotel in a garden that doubled the green-growing potential of its site.

5:04 · There’s now 15,000 meters of sky gardens, reflecting pools, waterfalls, planter terraces and green walls.

5:12 · And the government has big plans as well.

5:17 · Singapore actually has a plan to plant 1 million trees and add more green spaces over the next 10 years.

5:24 · It is actually a mix of one thing to reduce the urban heat island effect.

5:30 · But on the other hand, it’s also to to get the people to be more connected to nature.

5:39 · But it’s not enough.

Heat Mitigation

5:41 · The city-state has still been warming twice as quickly as the world average over the past six decades.

5:47 · That’s why Cooling Singapore has developed a catalog of other potential heat-mitigation measures.

5:54 · When you try to mitigate the urban heat island effect in a city or in any building, in a village as well, the first place to start is by shading of the windows.

6:05 · You have to keep areas clear so that the wind can move through it.

6:11 · Water of a certain depth can act as a very good thermal buffer.

6:15 · If you have to construct heavy buildings, such as high rises, at least you can make the surface, the facade less heavy.

6:24 · And you can protect it from direct sun penetration.

6:27 · We have to make sure that no combustion engines will be in the city in the middle, in the medium- to long-term range.

6:35 · So ideally, the electricity production is outside of the city.

6:39 · And you bring just the clean electricity into the city.

6:43 · You can at least minimize the use of energy in the city.

6:48 · And you can start to slowly convert the roofs, the facades of the city, into production areas for renewable energy.

6:58 · In Singapore, unfortunately, this is a limited option.

7:01 · But in the long run, it could produce up to 20%, 25% of the energy, of the electricity needed in Singapore.

7:10 · If all the roofs and the areas in the buildings, on the buildings, on the facades would be used to do that.

Urban Climate Twin

7:20 · With so many different ideas, Cooling Singapore is also designing a virtual model of the city to test them out.

7:26 · It’s called a Digital Urban Climate Twin, or DUCT, that will calculate how each element of the city’s design will impact the urban heat island effect.

7:37 · That means we model not only the geometry of the buildings digitally, but also we model the transportation, the insulation, the temperature, the radiation coming from the sun, the weather, the local weather, the local climate, the even very, very microclimate of the city, the water, the movement of people in the city.

8:01 · We can invent scenarios.

8:04 · We can design scenarios, test them before we actually build them.

8:09 · And if they test very well and we are sure that they will function, then we can start to build them and put them into reality.

Conclusion

8:23 · Singapore will be using this new tool to figure out which actions it should take next.

8:28 · And the model can be applied to any city, whether it needs to keep heat out or keep heat in, which will ultimately save energy, slow climate change and improve our quality of life.

8:41 · So this is something that Singapore will be able to export, maybe even together with its city development systems that it already has.

8:51 · Singapore is one of the very few cities in the world that really combined this scientific approach with a very well-established urban redesign and urban design approach.

9:03 · Through its agencies and the combination of its agencies, it has achieved a lot in the past.

9:10 · If it keeps following the scientific path and the combination with the other knowledge in the city already, we think that it will be a very comfortable and very livable city in the future, even more than today.

The urban heat island effect is a phenomenon affecting more than 100 cities across the U.S. — here’s how it works and why it disproportionately impacts communities of color.

How urban heat islands make the impacts of excessive heat worse

PBS NewsHour, 07 27, 2023

Video (6:11)
Description

Living in certain parts of a city can make the impacts of extreme heat worse. That’s because of a phenomenon called the urban heat island effect. A recent report by the research group Climate Central showed that more than 40 million Americans live in these hot spots. William Brangham discussed what this means for those residents with Michael Mendez of the University of California, Irvine.

Stream your PBS favorites with the PBS app: https://to.pbs.org/2Jb8twG

Find more from PBS NewsHour at https://www.pbs.org/newshour

Subscribe to our YouTube channel: https://bit.ly/2HfsCD6

Follow us:

TikTok: https://www.tiktok.com/@pbsnews

Twitter: http://www.twitter.com/newshour

Instagram: http://www.instagram.com/newshour

Facebook: http://www.pbs.org/newshour

Subscribe:

PBS NewsHour podcasts: https://www.pbs.org/newshour/podcasts

Newsletters: https://www.pbs.org/newshour/subscribe

Transcript
What is an urban heat island

0:00 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: So, in addition to the dangers faced by where someone works, sometimes, where you live can also make the impacts of extreme heat worse, like living in certain parts of a city.

0:10 · That’s because of a phenomenon called the urban heat island effect. A recent report by the research group Climate Central showed that more than 40 million Americans live in these hot spots.

0:22 · So, what does this mean for those residents? And is there any way that they can get relief?

0:26 · For that we turn to Michael Mendez. He’s assistant professor of environmental planning and policy at the University of California, Irvine, Professor Mendez, very good to have you on the “NewsHour.”

0:38 · Can you help us understand, what is the urban heat island effect?

0:43 · MICHAEL MENDEZ, University of California, Irvine: Thank you for having me here today.

0:45 · It’s a pleasure to be here to talk to you about this important issue that is affecting many people across the nation of – 40 percent of residents in the United States are under a heat advisory.

0:56 · And many of them are living in urban areas that don’t have adequate infrastructure, let alone environmental amenities like trees and other forms of greenery that provide shade. So, an urban heat island effect is where you have a lot of urbanized and suburban areas that are paved over with asphalt, concrete, other – other types of building materials that absorb the heat.

1:20 · And with little vegetation and trees for shade, and other types of cooling material, these areas and communities could be anywhere between five and even 20 degrees hotter than other neighborhoods that have more green space for shade.

1:36 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: That is such a shocking difference in temperature, simply that can be driven by how a certain area is built and what the structures are made of?

1:45 · MICHAEL MENDEZ: Most definitely.

1:47 · I think many of us that are millennials, Gen Z or even older remember being on the hot turf, asphalt, in schools and see the steam actually permeate out of these hard heat-trapping surfaces. So imagine not just a schoolyard, which is just, unfortunately, an urban heat island, but an entire neighborhood, and how that can really change how you experience an extreme heat event, and let alone if you’re living in an older home that’s 100 years old.

2:19 · You may experience it even more inside of a home that isn’t climate-resilient, can have even hotter temperatures than the ambient air outside.

2:29 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: I cited this study that says that millions of Americans are living in – under these types of conditions that you’re describing.

Can you explain why certain people are more vulnerable

2:36 · But I also understand that it is not equally spread across demographics, that certain people are more vulnerable than others. Can you explain?

2:46 · MICHAEL MENDEZ: Yes, this phenomenon of the heat waves, extreme climate events have disparate impact. It does not affect everyone equally.

2:55 · And this is because a heat wave is a natural phenomenon – phenomenon in some part, but there’s been political choices have been – have been made over the decades, if not centuries, that have withheld vital resources and infrastructure from communities, primarily low income-communities of color, African American and Latino communities.

3:16 · So it’s no surprise, when a heat wave or other type of extreme weather event strikes these communities, they’re the least prepared and often suffered the most impacts, because they don’t have – they have crumbling infrastructure, and they don’t have the amenities that will protect them and make them more resilient from our changing and extreme climate.

What are the possible solutions

3:36 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: You have been describing or hinting at a few of what seem like the possible solutions here.

3:42 · Walk me through those things. If I were a city planner, what are the things that I can do to try to reduce the urban heat island effect?

3:50 · MICHAEL MENDEZ: First is, a lot of these campaigns, we hear about the million-dollar campaigns that a lot of urban mayors are trying to institute in their cities, planting trees.

4:02 · Climate-resilient trees that are resilient to drought as well can help cool down are cities, cool roofs, rooftops, making sure that we’re able to reflect some of the sun’s rays and ensure that our houses that are cooler, having more climate weatherization in our homes, and, of course, having more cool pavements, moving away from that black asphalt and concrete that is absorbing and retaining the heat.

4:32 · And other types of green spaces, watershed management practices are multibenefit.

4:38 · They can clean our water, recharge our groundwater, but also provide green space and recreational spaces. So, we need to look for multibenefit policies and projects that attack multiple problems that climate change is causing our urban areas and throughout our nation.

What communities are doing

4:56 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: I mean, some of these things you’re talking about seem quite quick. I mean, you can paint a roof a different color quite quickly. Trees, we know, take longer to grow.

5:04 · Are there communities that you would point to in the country that are doing some of these things, that are taking this initiative head on and trying to do these things?

5:15 · MICHAEL MENDEZ: Well, in my in my hometown of Los Angeles, it’s often named – thought of as very suburban, very asphalt, concrete-driven area.

5:24 · But they’re really leading the way in terms of creating an Office of Climate Emergency and Resilience, where they have hired a full-time person and staff to understand these issues and try to target resources to the most immediate communities, those that are lacking these trees and green spaces.

5:44 · So, places like Los Angeles, Portland, of course, Seattle have been at the forefront of enacting climate action, but also having a strong equity and justice lens.

5:58 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: All right, Professor Michael Mendez, U.C. Irvine, thank you so much for being here.

6:02 · MICHAEL MENDEZ: Thank you for having me.

Inside a Category 5 Hurricane Simulator

Be Smart, 06 02, 2022

↓↓↓ More info and sources below ↓↓↓

Hurricanes, typhoons, and tropical cyclones are Earth’s most powerful storms, capable of unleashing destruction and death on coastal areas worldwide. As climate change warms Earth’s oceans, we face more risk of storms rapidly intensifying into category 5, sometimes in less than a single day. Being able to predict these rapidly intensifying storms will save lives, but studying hurricanes in detail is difficult and dangerous. So in order to improve our understanding of hurricanes, scientists have built a machine that can create hurricane conditions indoors. weather hurricanes

Filmed at the Alfred C. Glassell, Jr. SUSTAIN Laboratory at the Rosenstiel School of Marine and Atmospheric Science at the University of Miami https://sustain.rsmas.miami.edu/

Hurricanes can grow from dangerous to potentially historic levels of destruction in basically no time flat.

Atmosphere and ocean

7:19 · So being able to predict several days in advance whether a hurricane will be powerful could allow more time for evacuation and save lives. This storm machine is already helping scientists better understand how hurricanes grow. As a hurricane’s winds blow over the waves, the rough surface of the ocean exerts a drag on the storm, sucking energy out of it similar to how friction slows down a sliding object by removing its kinetic energy.

7:49 · What we’d expect is as a hurricane gets stronger, rougher seas, and bigger waves would suck out even more energy, preventing storms from growing too quickly. But what they’ve shown using the hurricane simulator is when wind reaches a certain high speed, that drag levels off and stops slowing the storm down.

8:09 · When you blow the wind, it makes these huge waves. The energy that was in the wind went into making those waves. As the winds get really intense, you might get the wind not really interacting as much.

8:20 · [Joe] It almost feels like if I was in a car and I started going really fast, that all of a sudden my tires would stop sticking to the road as well as they did when I was going slowly.

8:31 · Exactly.

8:32 · That’s a good way of looking at it. And all of a sudden the friction just disappears on your tires and– [Joe] As soon as you hit 60 miles an hour, it’s like, oh, the road turned to ice. Findings like these are already helping scientists create more accurate models that can predict earlier and more accurately when hurricanes will rapidly intensify. What makes this giant hurricane lab so special isn’t just the intense winds or crashing waves.

It will make energy dissipate so that the waves are weaker when they reach our shoreline so we are protected by these structures.

Mexico’s Caribbean beaches overwhelmed by record sargassum influx I Inside Latin America

Al Jazeera English, 09 05, 2026

Video (10:02)
Description

The Caribbean is facing a growing sargassum seaweed crisis, with huge quantities washing onto beaches across the region and threatening tourism, ecosystems and public health.

Mexico’s Riviera Maya has been particularly affected, with the influx reaching thousands of tonnes of seaweed each day.

As the algae decomposes on beaches, it can release toxic gases, raising health concerns for residents and tourists. Scientists also warn that the accumulation of sargassum can damage seagrass ecosystems and affect beach stability.

The crisis is putting pressure on Mexico’s tourism industry, while the Mexican Navy has stepped up efforts to collect the seaweed before it reaches the shore.

But some businesses are looking at sargassum differently.

Companies including Carbonwave and Sargablock are developing ways to transform the invasive algae into products such as bio-fertilizers, footwear and construction materials.

Could an environmental crisis become a new source of sustainable materials?

NASA Urban Heat Islands

NASA Goddard, 12 10, 2010

Video (2:23)
Description

At the 2010 American Geophysical Union (AGU) conference in San Francisco, Dr. Ping Zhang will present her findings on the Urban Heat Island effect. The data collected spans from 1995 to 2005 and will give scientists an idea of why cities are warmer than their surrounding areas, as well as what effect this will have on the planet.

This video is public domain and can be downloaded at: ‪http://svs.gsfc.nasa.gov/goto?10699

‪http://svs.gsfc.nasa.gov/vis/iTunes/f0004_index.html‬

‪http://www.facebook.com/NASA.GSFC‬

Or find us on Twitter:

‪http://twitter.com/NASAGoddard‬

Transcript
Intro

0:08 · Urban heat islands affect human health and it affects energy consumption. Those two things alone are enough to drive worldwide interest. (Zhang) When we build an urban area, we’re replacing the vegetated surface and also the soil surface with this imperious surface like the paving material and the building material. By doing this, we’re basically warming the urban areas, and this will generate a temperature difference.

What is Urban Heat Island

0:36 · And that’s what we can the urban heat island. We find out that the urban heat island affected by several factors.

Factors affecting Urban Heat Island

0:44 · The first factor will be the surrounding ecological context.

0:48 · And then will be the size of the city. When talking about size, we can we mean both the area of the city and also the population size of the city. And then will be the shape of the city and also the development patterns of the city.

Urban Heat Island

1:04 · (Imhoff) We used a variety of satellite data, both land surface data from MODIS and also impervious surface data from the Landsat satellite. Inpervious surface data essentially tells us how much building material is on the land surface in order to study the urban heat island. The urban heat island is much larger if you convert a forested area into an urban. And this is because the urban heat island is a relative measure. So, urban areas in forests are much warmer than the surrounding landscape than they are in deserts for example, because the surrounding landscape is already warm.

1:40 · I think the general public should be interested in urban heat islands because of the fact that it’s where most of the people live and in the next 50 years, we’re going to see 80% of the global population living in cities. And the urban heat island matters for everything from health, like asthma and heart conditions, to how much heating and air conditioning you need to use to cool or heat your living space.

In the foothills of New Zealand’s Mt Aoraki, Dr Ulyana Horodyskyj Peña collects snow samples in search of black carbon; dense particulate matter from human activity which darkens the snow and ice surfaces, leading to more absorbed solar energy and ultimately amplifying melt.

Drivers of rapid spread

Wind and ignition are difficult to change and control, but there’s a piece of the fast fire puzzle that’s a key force in their rise: dry, receptive fuel.

6:14 · We’ve had fuels that have been drying out all summer long and then we just throw in a little weather and a fire. And those are mixtures and that recipe for extreme fire behavior.

6:23 · And firefighters even have a term for this fire weather. At that point, fire can rip through dry fuels and spread extremely fast. In the U.S. we use the more general term red flag conditions.

6:46 · So what does that mean for the future of fast fires?

Climate change and risk

6:50 · With increasing temperatures, we know that we’re essentially pulling more moisture out of fuels, we’re making them drier. And that’s the big impact of climate change on fire is making fuels more available and more available throughout the year.

7:05 · We’re seeing extreme weather patterns change and and intensify across the globe. We’re getting all sorts of situations in which weather patterns are driving extreme fire behavior.

7:13 · Models predict that the number of extreme fire spread events will more than double with two degrees Celsius of global warming, plus a doubling of acres burned. And that’s not far from our current level of warming. We can’t have a fast fire without strong wind. The danger of wind events

7:35 · We worked with NASA to create scientifically accurate animations of the wind and fire progressions during a handful of fast, destructive fires to illustrate what’s going on.

7:45 · I’m hard pressed to think of an event that destroyed more than a hundred homes that didn’t have wind involved.

7:51 · And the common denominator for us to make it hard to control wildfires is wind.

7:55 · When strong winds meet or start a wildfire, they throw embers miles ahead of the flaming front, starting new fires and spreading the wildfire outward. These conditions supercharged the East Troublesome Fire. The fire was so fast, moving at 18 miles in a single day, that it jumped The Continental Divide, an area with very little fuel for fire. In the Marshall Fire, we can see a type of wind that’s particularly dangerous: down slope winds.

8:23 · As wind drops in elevation, atmospheric pressure increases, which dries and warms the air. Temperatures can increase by as much as 5.5 degrees Fahrenheit for every 1000 feet of lost elevation, drying vegetation, and eliminating the need for high temperatures with wildfire. The fire started on December 30th, just outside of Boulder, Colorado.

8:46 · Wind gusts of up to 115 miles per hour flowed off the front range of the Rocky Mountains, sucking any remaining moisture out of the vegetation to create the most destructive fire in state history. 9:13 · During the Labor Day fires, river canyons turned into wind tunnels with speeds reaching 100 miles per hour. Wind blew over power lines and ignited fires in and around small communities, fanning existing fires to create one of the most destructive fire seasons in state history. These aren’t just regular wildfires, they’re wind events with fire in them.

Preparing for future fires

9:50 · And that’s probably the most important lesson about fast fires. Once they’ve started, there’s no stopping them until the wind dies down. Firefighters can only evacuate people and wait. Trains, planes and automobiles The state of U.S. infrastructure and why it’s hard to build things

Harvard Kennedy School, 11 25, 2025

Video (6:07)
Description

The state of U.S. infrastructure is improving, but not great, according to Samantha Silverberg MPP 2012, a former Biden administration official and visiting fellow at the Taubman Center for State and Local Government at Harvard Kennedy School. In this video, Silverberg discusses the challenges that face federal, state and local government officials who endeavor to fix the country’s infrastructure, from roads and bridges to tap water and transmission lines. Topics covered include:

• The importance of infrastructure to our daily lives

• Why it’s often challenging to get new infrastructure built

• What infrastructure priorities policymakers should focus on in the coming years.

You can read more about Ms. Silverberg’s thoughts on our website: https://www.hks.harvard.edu/centers/taubman/state-and-local-perspectives/interview-samantha-silverberg-mpp-2012-taubman-center

About Harvard Kennedy School:

The John F. Kennedy School of Government at Harvard University is a graduate and professional school that brings together students, scholars, and practitioners who combine thought and action to make the world a better place.

Our mission is to improve public policy and public leadership across the United States and around the world so that people can lead safer, freer, and more prosperous lives. Harvard Kennedy School teaches current and future leaders the skills they need to effectively advance the public purpose in the public, nonprofit, and private sectors. Our renowned faculty and trailblazing research centers pioneer bold new ideas. And as the most international school at Harvard, we convene global leaders in the Forum, host visiting experts in the classroom, and attract a diverse community of faculty, students, and staff.

The importance of infrastructure

0:00 · Listen, right now it takes too long and it costs too much to build infrastructure in the United States, and we need to do something about that.

0:10 · Infrastructure is so important for everything we do in our daily lives.

0:15 · Think about the roads you drove on or the train that you took to get to school or work today, the tap you turned on and the water you drank, the internet that you use, even the electricity that powers everything in our homes.

0:28 · But infrastructure is something that we really only pay attention to when it fails.

Current state and challenges

0:33 · The state of infrastructure in the US right now is better than it was, but still not great.

0:40 · The American Society of Civil Engineers gave US infrastructure a grade of C in its most recent infrastructure report card, and that’s really because of decades of underinvestment in our infrastructure that has led to crumbling roads and bridges, unreliable transit systems, power networks that are unable to withstand the impacts of climate change and so much more.

1:06 · There are tens of thousands of bridges in this country that are in poor condition.

1:12 · There are millions of Americans that still don’t have access to high-speed internet, which is far worse in rural America.

2:03 · You could think about the Hoover Dam, the Bay Bridge, LaGuardia Airport.

2:12 · These were all built during the New Deal.

Overcoming bureaucracy

2:43 · That process can be exploited by actors who are trying to stop good things from getting done.

2:50 · The system right now is overly litigious.

2:53 · Things like environmental permitting, which have an important and good purpose, have been used as a tool to stop good projects, even clean energy projects that have an overall positive benefit on the environment.

Investing in public capacity

4:23 · On the mega project side, one of the examples that many people raise is the Mario Cuomo Bridge in New York.

4:29 · This was the replacement of the Tappan Zee Bridge.

4:31 · It was executed using an innovative procurement method at the time.

4:36 · It also went through an expedited environmental permitting process, and as a result of both of those activities, it was delivered on time, on budget, and, by some estimates, saved billions of dollars for the taxpayer.

4:49 · At the same time, I think it’s really important to think about those smaller backyard projects.

4:55 · In the Biden Administration, one of our priorities was to replace lead pipes.

4:59 · There are still 9 million lead pipes serving our homes, which are a source of toxic chemicals.

5:05 · Through money provided by the federal government, the city of Milwaukee has already replaced thousands of lead pipes.

Future priorities

5:12 · I think the top three infrastructure priorities for the next 10 years are, number one, climate resilience.

5:18 · We need to protect our communities from the impacts of climate change.

5:21 · The second is clean energy generation and transmission so that we could power our future.

5:26 · And the third is to close the digital divide, because we know that affordable, reliable high-speed internet is important for everything from remote school to work to accessing telehealth.

5:37 · I also want to say, though, that every community knows what their own priorities are and they can’t just be told by Washington where they need to invest.

5:45 · And so the feds need to continue to invest in infrastructure carrying forward the legacy of the 2021 Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act so that our communities can invest in what they need.

Urbanization and the future of cities - Vance Kite

TED-Ed, 09 12, 2013

Video (4:08)
Description

View full lesson: http://ed.ted.com/lessons/urbanization-and-the-future-of-cities-vance-kite

About 10,000 years ago, hunter-gatherers, aided by rudimentary agriculture, moved to semi-permanent villages and never looked back. With further developments came food surpluses, leading to commerce, specialization and, many years later with the Industrial Revolution, the modern city. Vance Kite plots our urban past and how we can expect future cities to adapt to our growing populations.

Lesson by Vance Kite, animation by ATMG Studio.

Transcript

0:06 · Today ,more than half of all people in the world live in an urban area.

0:11 · By mid-century, this will increase to 70%.

0:14 · But as recently as 100 years ago, only two out of ten people lived in a city, and before that, it was even less.

0:21 · How have we reached such a high degree of urbanization, and what does it mean for our future?

0:27 · In the earliest days of human history, humans were hunter-gatherers, often moving from place to place in search of food.

0:35 · But about 10,000 years ago, our ancestors began to learn the secrets of selective breeding and early agricultural techniques.

0:42 · For the first time, people could raise food rather than search for it, and this led to the development of semi-permanent villages for the first time in history.

0:51 · “Why only semi-permanent?” you might ask.

0:54 · Well, at first, the villages still had to relocate every few years as the soil became depleted.

1:00 · It was only with the advent of techniques like irrigation and soil tilling about 5,000 years ago that people could rely on a steady and long-term supply of food, making permanent settlements possible.

1:11 · And with the food surpluses that these techniques produced, it was no longer necessary for everyone to farm.

1:17 · This allowed the development of other specialized trades, and, by extension, cities.

1:23 · With cities now producing surplus food, as well as tools, crafts, and other goods, there was now the possibility of commerce and interaction over longer distances.

1:33 · And as trade flourished, so did technologies that facilitated it, like carts, ships, roads, and ports.

1:43 · Of course, these things required even more labor to build and maintain, so more people were drawn from the countryside to the cities as more jobs and opportunities became available.

1:52 · If you think modern cities are overcrowded, you may be surprised to learn that some cities in 2000 B.C. had population densities nearly twice as high as that of Shanghai or Calcutta.

2:03 · One reason for this was that transportation was not widely available, so everything had to be within walking distance, including the few sources of clean water that existed then.

2:13 · And the land area of the city was further restricted by the need for walls to defend against attacks.

2:20 · The Roman Empire was able to develop infrastructure to overcome these limitations, but other than that, modern cities as we know them, didn’t really get their start until the Industrial Revolution, when new technology deployed on a mass scale allowed cities to expand and integrate further, establishing police, fire, and sanitation departments, as well as road networks, and later electricity distribution.

Industrial Revolution
What Is the Future of Cities

2:43 · So, what is the future of cities?

2:46 · Global population is currently more than 7 billion and is predicted to top out around 10 billion.

2:52 · Most of this growth will occur in the urban areas of the world’s poorest countries.

2:57 · So, how will cities need to change to accommodate this growth?

3:01 · First, the world will need to seek ways to provide adequate food, sanitation, and education for all people.

3:07 · Second, growth will need to happen in a way that does not damage the land that provides us with the goods and services that support the human population.

3:17 · Food production might move to vertical farms and skyscrapers, rooftop gardens, or vacant lots in city centers, while power will increasingly come from multiple sources of renewable energy.

3:28 · Instead of single-family homes, more residences will be built vertically.

3:33 · We may see buildings that contain everything that people need for their daily life, as well as a smaller, self-sufficient cities focused on local and sustainable production.

The Future of Cities

3:42 · The future of cities is diverse, malleable, and creative, no longer built around a single industry, but reflecting an increasingly connected and global world.

What Does Extreme Heat Do to the Body

PBS, 09 03, 2026

Video (9:59)
Description

Watch more of this series: https://to.pbs.org/4wkQIAG

The climate crisis isn’t a distant threat — it’s already here, and it’s deadly. But keeping people cool is surprisingly complicated.

In Sydney, Australia, physiologist Ollie Jay is tackling the problem by studying how the human body responds to extreme heat. The ultimate goal is to create low-cost cooling strategies for people without access to air conditioning — those most at risk from extreme heat. Science journalist Miles O’Brien puts himself in the hot seat — literally — to find out what it feels like when the body’s cooling system starts to fail.

Resolve to Solve with Miles O’Brien

Miles O’Brien travels the globe searching for solutions to climate, water, and health crises. From building sea curtains in Antarctica to restoring wildlife in Argentina, the answers are closer than we think thanks to people with a “Resolve to Solve.”

Transcript

What is Climate Change

CrashCourse, 08 15, 2023

Video (13:57)
Description

Life on Earth has weathered boiling-hot oceans and volcanic-ash-darkened skies—but that’s nothing like the climate change we’re experiencing now. In this episode of Crash Course Biology, we’ll talk about the greenhouse effect, learn why our climate is like a tangled pair of headphones, and discover that we’ve understood the science behind climate change for much longer than you might think.

The Greenhouse Effect 2:24

Measuring the Greenhouse Effect 4:46

Carbon Sinks 8:26
