Transcript
Introduction to Climate Change

0:00 · Our planet has been draped in ice sheets, filled with boiling-hot oceans, and dimmed by volcanic ash – all before anything more complicated than a single-celled organism showed up.

0:10 · So climate change is nothing new around here… when it happens gradually, over millions of years.

0:17 · But something new has happened in the last few centuries.

0:20 · People like us…except, wearing hats like this… began burning fossil fuels like coal, oil, and natural gas to make energy.

0:29 · Most of us aren’t wearing hats like those anymore.

0:31 · But we’re still powering our daily lives and industries with those fuels, releasing billions of tons of carbon dioxide every year.

0:41 · And that’s caused Earth’s climate to change in the span of just a few human lifetimes, the geological blink of an eye.

0:48 · Hi! I’m Dr. Sammy, your friendly neighborhood entomologist, and this is Crash Course Biology!

0:56 · Hey, do you guys smell that?

0:58 · …smells like…

1:00 · theme music?

1:01 · [THEME MUSIC] Now, I know what you’re thinking.

1:12 · “Wait, isn’t this Crash Course Biology?”

1:15 · And yeah, this episode is heavy on the gases, low on the golgi bodies.

1:19 · And that might leave you wondering, what does climate change have to do with the science of life?

1:25 · Here’s the thing: life and climate are tied together, like that tangled pair of headphones at the bottom of your backpack.

1:30 · You know, like you tug one end and then a knot tightens, which is looped around a paperclip, somehow snagged on that tiny notebook where you drew hearts around your crush’s name, which after several tugs is now lying open on the floor and…I’m breaking into a cold sweat just thinking about it.

1:46 · But back to the point, we can’t talk about life without talking about climate —which doesn’t mean last week’s thunderstorm or a one-day temperature swing – that’s weather.

1:58 · Climate is long-term weather conditions averaged over many years.

2:03 · To understand the difference between them, just remember that knowing the weather will help you decide if you should grab an umbrella before you head out, but knowing the climate will help you decide if you should invest in a good air conditioner.

2:15 · While the weather might impact your choice of clothing for the day, the climate directly impacts where and when different kinds of life can survive.

The Greenhouse Effect

2:24 · We owe today’s climate to the fact that our little green-and-blue marble of a home isn’t just floating in space unprotected.

2:31 · It’s wrapped up in an atmosphere; a big, invisible, gassy jacket.

2:35 · Which, granted, sounds pretty weird when you put it that way.

2:38 · But without it, we wouldn’t exist.

2:40 · This jacket is made of different kinds of gases.

2:43 · And a small fraction of them, known as greenhouse gases, absorb solar energy like, super well.

2:49 · We’re talking about gases like methane, water vapor, and most importantly carbon dioxide – also known as CO2.

2:58 · They account for less than half of one percent of our atmospheric jacket.

3:03 · But they’re a part of what makes it so good at trapping heat, sort of like all the little white feathers in your puffy coat.

3:09 · When sunlight beams down from space, most of it travels through those gases with no problem.

3:14 · The energy from that sunlight gets absorbed as it strikes the Earth, warming the surface.

3:19 · That type of warming is normal and seasonal.

3:22 · The Earth naturally bounces some of that solar energy back toward space.

3:27 · Where some of it exits the atmosphere and heads right back out into the inky ether.

3:32 · But the rest of that energy gets trapped by the Earth’s gassy jacket.

3:36 · Specifically by those super absorbent greenhouse gases, which suck up heat and bounce it back down to us again.

3:44 · This warming process is called the greenhouse effect.

3:47 · Unsurprisingly, It works the same way as a greenhouse – using layers of glass to trap heat inside.

3:54 · And it keeps Earth at a nice, cozy, insulated average of 14 degrees Celsius.

4:00 · Without it, our Earth would be a chilly average of -18 degrees Celsius.

4:06 · Great for storing ice cream!

4:08 · Not so good for rainforests, swimsuits, or us for that matter.

4:13 · So, the greenhouse effect is a natural, helpful process that makes Earth habitable for all of life!

4:18 · But you can have too much of a good thing.

4:21 · When our atmospheric jacket contains more carbon dioxide, for example, it gets really good at trapping heat.

4:28 · Like, too good.

4:30 · And the hotter things get, the more water evaporates and joins the atmosphere— and remember, water vapor itself is a greenhouse gas, so that in turn absorbs even more heat, creating a looping system of cause-and-effect that just keeps reinforcing itself.

Measuring the Greenhouse Effect

4:46 · And while dressing in layers is great if you’re hiking in the Alps, it’s really hard for our planet to shed its extra coats.

4:52 · So all of that heat gets stuck going from the ground to the atmosphere like the worst game of hot potato ever played.

4:59 · When I learned about this, I was like, “Wow, what a revelation!

5:03 · I can’t believe we’ve only recently figured this out!”

5:05 · But it turns out that this isn’t new knowledge; we’ve known how, and why, this could happen for nearly 170 years.

5:16 · Let’s pay a visit to the Theater of Life… [Inquisitive music] Back in 1856, Eunice Foote, an American scientist and suffragette, was thinking about how the Sun’s warmth affected different gases.

5:27 · In those days, the scientific community was a bit like a fort with a handmade “no girls allowed” sign out front.

5:34 · But Foote wasn’t deterred, and ran her experiments anyway.

5:38 · She filled tubes with different combinations of gases, including carbon dioxide.

5:42 · After putting some tubes in the Sun and some in the shade, she compared their temperatures, trying to find the hottest gas.

5:50 · All the tubes in direct sunlight warmed up.

5:52 · But none as intensely as the tube that contained carbon dioxide.

5:57 · The temperature had soared to 51.7 degrees Celsius, hot enough to burn your fingers.

6:04 · From that insight, Foote theorized that if our atmosphere ever contained more carbon dioxide, the whole planet would warm up as a result, which would mean a lot more than a few scalded fingers.

6:16 · And sure enough, today, that’s exactly the situation we’re in.

6:20 · Foote was one of the first scientists to recognize carbon dioxide’s potential to affect Earth’s climate.

6:25 · But she wasn’t the only one to connect the dots.

6:28 · For example, just a few decades later, another scientist named Dr. Svante Arrhenius observed that burning coal releases carbon dioxide.

6:37 · As a fossil fuel, coal forms from the decomposed, carbon-based bodies of plants and animals that lived and died a long time ago.

6:46 · And I’m talking before the dinosaurs.

6:48 · So no, I’m afraid that means you aren’t gassing up your car with the remains of a T-Rex.

6:53 · When we burn those fossil fuels— whether it’s in the form of coal, oil, or natural gas— carbon dioxide gets released into the atmosphere.

7:00 · When Arrhenius ran the numbers, he predicted that carbon dioxide released by burning fossil fuels could warm our climate within a few thousand years if we kept burning them at their current rate.

7:14 · But we’ve far outpaced his estimates.

7:16 · Our emissions of carbon dioxide have grown and grown and grown — and so has the mountain of evidence that those emissions are warming our planet.

7:25 · Some oil and gas companies have worked to promote uncertainty around the existence of climate change and what’s causing it.

7:32 · While they have only very recently acknowledged its existence, as of 2023, they’re still trying to deflect from what’s causing it.

7:40 · But the scientific consensus on this is overwhelming.

7:44 · You can learn much more about that in our Climate & Energy series.

7:47 · We’ve only been reliably taking direct measurements of Earth’s global temperature since the 19th century.

7:52 · But that measly slice of time shows a steady rise in temperature of about 1.1 degree Celsius since 1880.

8:01 · And we know that that’s unusual because we’ve learned to read Earth’s much longer climate diary, in the form of ice cores.

8:07 · See, when snow hardens into ice, tiny bubbles of air remain trapped in the gaps between snowflakes.

8:13 · These gases and water molecules stay frozen, like entries in a frosty journal.

8:19 · So by drilling deep down into polar ice, we can snoop on what the atmosphere was like hundreds of thousands of years ago.

Carbon Sinks

8:27 · Ice cores show us that carbon dioxide levels have fluctuated over the past 800,000 years.

8:32 · And temperatures have fallen and risen alongside them, too.

8:36 · But when people started burning fossil fuels, carbon dioxide levels began to spike quickly, like really, really quickly.

8:44 · And they haven’t stopped rising.

8:45 · Before the Industrial Revolution, for every million molecules of air in our atmosphere, around 280 were carbon dioxide molecules.

8:54 · But by 2022, that number had increased to 422 —the highest concentration of CO2 our planet has seen in 4 million years.

9:06 · And that surge in carbon dioxide affects more than just the temperature.

9:10 · As the amount of carbon dioxide in our atmosphere rises, it impacts all of Earth’s systems.

9:15 · Just like yanking on the end of that tangled mess of headphones impacted all of the other items in my bag (and eventually, everybody around us when stuff started falling out).

9:24 · It’s all connected, is what I’m saying.

9:26 · For example, the ocean is our planet’s largest carbon sink.

9:31 · It’s sort of like a big storage container for carbon.

9:34 · In fact, the ocean holds 50 times more carbon than the air or soil do.

9:40 · But a chemical reaction happens when carbon dioxide meets water, it creates an acid.

9:45 · So that influx of carbon has already turned the ocean 30% more acidic since the 19th century.

9:53 · As carbon dioxide and other greenhouse gases trap more heat, there’s more energy pouring into our planet than going out.

9:59 · That means more energy is pumped into the ocean, fueling hurricanes and typhoons to become more frequent and more intense.

Environmental Justice

10:07 · As the whole planet gets hotter, that triggers all kinds of changes.

10:11 · Spring arrives earlier, leading to shorter winters and longer summers.

10:15 · Rising temperatures lead to double-whammy droughts and heatwaves.

10:19 · And that leaves forest floors full of dried-up plants that serve as fuel for wildfires to ignite— spreading faster, farther, and more often.

10:28 · Plus, Earth’s polar ice caps are melting, transforming solid ice to slush and seawater.

10:34 · And that’s causing ocean levels to rise and encroach on land.

10:38 · So while some communities are already facing a problem of not enough water, others are facing a problem of too much.

10:45 · And because these adverse changes layer on top of existing social inequalities, they disproportionately affect lower-income communities and people of color, making climate change not only a scientific issue, but a matter of environmental justice that has spurred some researchers to political action.

11:02 · For example, climate scientist Nicole Hernandez Hammer witnessed first-hand, through her field research, the sea-level rise alongside Miami Beach’s Latino communities.

11:12 · But these communities were not included in conversations about climate change or the dangers of rising sea levels.

11:18 · So, Hammer took action, moving into environmental outreach and education.

11:23 · And, while the threat is still there, these communities are now in conversation about climate change, and able to plan and advocate for the future of their environment.

11:33 · And the good news more broadly is we know exactly why these tangled, complex effects are happening.

11:39 · The more we burn those fossil fuels, the more greenhouse gases we release —and that’s driving sweeping changes all over our planet.

11:47 · And these changes are impacting life at all levels from the tiniest bacterium, to the elephants of Botswana, to you and me.

11:55 · We can slow these impacts by breaking up with fossil fuels.

11:58 · But we also have to invest in nature’s carbon sinks —such as soils and forests— which pull carbon out of the atmosphere and back into the land, where it can’t keep heating things up.

Review & Credits

12:09 · It won’t be the easiest breakup.

12:12 · We’ve designed whole societies and global systems around fossil fuels.

12:16 · We should fully expect to be listening to Jazmine Sullivan on repeat with a gallon of Rocky Road.

12:21 · But this is one relationship we can’t afford to stay in.

12:24 · So, in order to halt emissions, we have to both invent new systems and work more efficiently within old ones, and to do that, we’ve got to get a whole planet’s worth of people on board.

12:35 · And if you’ve ever been part of a group project, you know that last part isn’t easy.

12:39 · Thankfully, when faced with global crises, humans have one great thing going for us: we are creative.

12:46 · I mean, we’ve been to space, we’ve got electric cars, we made furbies for some reason.

12:53 · So yes, It’s going to take all of our creativity and cooperation to tackle.

12:58 · But the only way out is through, and the only way through is together.

13:04 · In our next episode, we’ll tune back into the world of living things— and see how our rapidly changing climate involves much more than the atmosphere.

13:12 · It affects every living, breathing, organism on this planet— including you and me.

When Will Extreme Heat Become Unlivable

PBS Terra, 06 18, 2025

Video (11:45)
Description

Check out our Patreon: https://www.patreon.com/cw/PBSWeathered?utm_medium=unknown&utm_source=join_link&utm_campaign=creatorshare_creator&utm_content=copyLink

Heat is the deadliest weather hazard in the U.S. and many places around the world, and it’s only getting worse.

The most deadly heat waves so far have been dry heat waves. But a new threat is rising: humid heat waves, aka wet-bulb events. Scientists have identified wet-bulb temperatures where sweat can’t evaporate fast enough to cool the human body. And once this threshold is crossed, it doesn’t matter how much shade or water you have: you won’t survive without environmental cooling like air conditioning.

In this episode of Weathered, we break down: What wet-bulb temperature actually means, why it’s so dangerous, what areas will be affected, and why air conditioning, while essential, isn’t enough.

Watch Walking With Dinosaurs Unearthed! https://youtu.be/E-jtwNGnQ5U?si=BXZZueP46YPDfgS6 ***** PBS Member Stations rely on viewers like you. To support your local station, go to: http://to.pbs.org/DonateTerra *****

Subscribe to PBS Terra so you never miss an episode! https://bit.ly/3mOfd77

And keep up with PBS Terra on:

Facebook: https://facebook.com/pbsdigitalstudios

Tiktok: https://www.tiktok.com/@pbsterra

Instagram: https://instagram.com/pbsterra

Transcript
The new era of extreme heat

0:00 · Most of the deadliest heat waves to date have been dry heat waves. But as our climate warms a possibly even deadlier heat is on the rise.

0:08 · It doesn’t matter how breezy it is, it doesn’t matter how much shade you’re under. It doesn’t matter how much water you’re drinking.

0:14 · These are extreme humid heat waves, often referred to as wet-bulb events. And these are, in my opinion, one of the scariest byproducts of climate change. They’ve been relatively rare historically, but a new study shows that by two degrees Celsius of global warming, many of the most populated places on Earth are likely to experience them. And the only way to protect ourselves from these dangerous wet-bulb temperatures is to cool our environment.

0:42 · But what happens if that’s not possible?

0:45 · The time of year that always has the highest stress on the grid is always just the hottest day of the year because so many people are using AC and so then the AC is liable to go out.

0:55 · What happens if there’s another extreme weather event?

0:57 · If you have a tropical cyclone that comes in, wipes out power?

1:00 · Stick around as we break down why these temperatures are so deadly and how we can prepare for a world that’s so warm, our bodies literally can’t handle it. Let’s start by talking about dry versus humid heat. Dry heat waves are usually driven by large high pressure systems that trap hot air near the surface. These systems push air downward, and as the air compresses, it heats up in a process called adiabatic heating.

Dry vs. humid heat waves

1:33 · If the high stays stuck in place, the heat builds day after day with no clouds or wind to carry it away. And that’s exactly what happened during the 2003 European heat wave and the 2021 Pacific Northwest heat dome. Now let’s take a look at humid heat.

1:49 · As temperatures rise, we can hold a lot more moisture in the atmosphere. And so this means that if a given location has air temperatures going up with ongoing climate change, it’s likely that if they have a moisture source, humidity’s also going to increase.

2:02 · We just saw how dangerous this can be in 2015 when one of the most extreme humid heat events on record struck Karachi, Pakistan. Temperatures up to 45 degrees Celsius combined with oppressive humidity, power outages and water shortages made conditions even worse. At least 1200 people died in just 10 days most from heat stroke and dehydration.

2:26 · Humans primarily cool down by sweating, but when the air is humid, sweat can’t evaporate as easily, so we don’t cool off as well. This is where wet-bulb comes into play. wet-bulb temperature combines heat and humidity into a single number, which helps us understand when conditions become dangerous or even lethal.

Understanding wet bulb

2:46 · You can imagine taking a normal thermometer. If you take that thermometer and you cover the bulb in a little sock that is then placed into a container of water, so it’s constantly wetted. Evaporation’s going to take place off of that little sock and it’s gonna have a net cooling effect.

3:03 · The wet sock at the end of the wet-bulb thermometer functions similarly to human skin. Water evaporates off the wet sock while sweat evaporates off our wet skin. In both cases, evaporation carries away heat into the surrounding environment.

3:18 · But the more humid the ambient air is, the more difficult and less efficient that evaporation is to take place. And so that makes any cooling through evaporative cooling, less efficient at high temperatures and high humidity.

3:31 · Which is why humid heat feels so muggy and uncomfortable for us and why it can be so dangerous. There have been a number of attempts to better understand how humid heat affects the human body, in particular where our physical limits are, but it’s not easy to identify dangerous wet-bulb thresholds because well, they’re dangerous.

Physiological limits of heat

3:53 · So obviously you can’t put someone in a heat chamber and truly cook them. That would be unethical. But what you can do is increase temperature and humidity and measure someone’s core temperature and see what’s the point at which their core temperature starts to increase. And that’s the limit of compensability.

4:11 · The human body generally does a good job of regulating its core body temperature through sweat. But once we cross the limit of livability or compensability, as scientists call it, our core body temperature starts to increase uncontrollably. And this wet-bulb temperature range is actually a lot lower than we previously believed.

4:33 · So this paper is really trying to summarize the most state-of-the-art estimations of these thresholds. And so what we see is that there is a range. So uncompensable wet-bulb temperatures range from about 19 to 32 degrees Celsius.

4:46 · And you might think the lower end of this range, 19 degrees Celsius sounds really low. But it’s important to understand that a 19 degree wet-bulb does not equal 19 degrees on a regular thermometer. So 19 degrees Celsius wet-bulb is actually more like 46 degrees Celsius with low humidity or 32 degrees Celsius with higher humidity.

5:09 · And the reason there’s a range is because this threshold depends on a number of variables including activity level, exact air temperature, and humidity levels and age.

5:22 · Older adults thermo regulatory responses are worse, they sweat less efficiently, they also have higher risks of comorbidities, so things like cardiovascular and respiratory diseases.

5:31 · So according to their study, where on earth are we likely to cross these thresholds?

Future global heat impact

5:37 · Well, at two degrees Celsius of global warming, which could come in as little as two decades for older adults. It’s about a third of Earth’s land area, but that only tells part of the story.

5:47 · It’s 35% of the land area, but it’s also many of our most populous places on earth. Most of India, most of the populated area of China. This is most of the eastern portion of the U.S.

6:01 · And if we let global warming get really out of hand or if climate sensitivity is just on the higher end of our projections at four degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels, over 60% of Earth’s landmass could see these dangerous wet-bulb temperatures. Heat is the deadliest weather disaster in the U.S. in many parts of the world, and it’s projected to get a lot deadlier.

The necessity of cooling

6:26 · During the 2003 European heatwave, up to 3,400 people died in Paris alone. At two degrees Celsius, which is again only a couple decades away, scientists found that Paris will see deadly heat waves like this once every few years rather than once a century. And though this heat wave in 2003 was among the deadliest in recent history, it didn’t get close to the dangerous wet-bulb thresholds we’ve been discussing in this episode.

6:54 · But the 2015 heat wave in Karachi did it passed the uncompensable wet-bulb threshold for older adults for something like eight to 10% of the time. But at two degrees Celsius of warming, we can see that the uncompensable threshold is crossed for younger adults as well. And at four degrees, models show that the same event would cross the uncompensable threshold for older adults for about 90% of the heat waves duration.

7:22 · And the thing to remember about these critical wet-bulb threshold is that once you cross one, the only way to bring your internal temperature down is by cooling yourself or your environment.

7:33 · It doesn’t matter how breezy it is, it doesn’t matter how much shade you’re under, it doesn’t matter how much water you’re drinking. The only way you can protect yourself from these conditions is by cooling your environment. And so that is why air conditioning is really the major adaptation measure and protection measure that we have.

Risks of relying on AC

7:51 · And although AC is one of the most important tools for surviving extreme heat, it’s far from perfect. First of all, it’s expensive. AC is energy intensive, and as our climate gets warmer, many people won’t be able to afford to stay cool. And due to its financial inaccessibility, many people in places most vulnerable to deadly heat don’t have access to AC. Second, air conditioning doesn’t eliminate heat, it just moves it.

8:20 · Cooling the indoors means transferring the heat outside, which warms the surrounding air. And in cities, this added heat can worsen the urban heat island effect. Now, let’s imagine that everyone could afford air conditioning. It still wouldn’t automatically solve the problem. Our power grids in the U.S. and globally weren’t designed for everyone to crank up their units at once. During heat waves, widespread outages become more likely leaving the most vulnerable at even greater risk.

8:53 · If AC units were our only way of protecting people as soon as the power system fails, whether that’s from the grid being overloaded, or if we have another weather driven sort of infrastructure damage that’s going to put people immediately at risk.

9:07 · And these compounding hazards like a heat wave after a hurricane are a growing concern as we head towards unprecedented levels of warming for humans. And as weather across the board just gets more extreme, but there’s one more catch. The more we rely on ac, the more we could be fueling the base problem of global warming.

Sustainable cooling solutions

9:29 · If you tried to just build out air conditioning to deal with heat risk, how much would that contribute to carbon emissions across the world?

9:38 · And how much would that positively feed back on global temperature making the temperature even higher?

9:44 · Right

9:44 · now, air conditioning accounts for about 3% of global greenhouse gas emissions. And according to the International Energy Agency, the number of AC units worldwide is expected to nearly triple by 2050 leading to a massive rise in energy demand.

10:01 · So what do we do about this paradox?

10:04 · Well, I think it’s a powerful argument for transitioning as quickly as possible to clean energy. The only way to meet the growing need for AC without accelerating climate change is to power our grids with renewables. And of course, there’s a lot we can and should do beyond just air conditioning. We can rethink the design of our cities by installing white or green roofs and planting trees.

10:30 · We can also build more cooling centers, upgrade our electrical grids to be more resilient and prioritize protecting the most vulnerable during heat waves. The truth is that we are headed into uncharted territory, but it’s also true that we still have time to choose how bad it gets.

Conclusion and resources

10:48 · So do you live in an area that’s already experiencing dangerously hot and humid conditions?

10:54 · What strategies have worked for you to handle the heat?

10:58 · Let us know in the comments and we’ll see you next time on Weathered. Oh, and before you go, we also wanted to let you know about something special PBS and the BBC have teamed up to Bring You Walking with Dinosaurs Unearthed. An amazing show about the prehistoric beast that ruled the Earth before us and experienced some pretty extreme weather of their own.

11:19 · The premiere episode just published earlier this week on PBS Terra, and we think it’s something our viewers will really enjoy. Click the link on screen or below to check it out and tell ’em Weathered sent you.

Why cities should plant more trees

Vox, 12 22, 2016

Video (3:10)
Description

Over 3 million people die annually from air pollution. Planting trees can help lower that number.

Read more: http://www.vox.com/energy-and-environment/2016/11/4/13510352/planting-trees-pollution-heat-waves

Check out The Nature Conservancy report, “Planting Healthy Air”: https://global.nature.org/content/healthyair

///

Trees help improve public health by cleaning and cooling the air around them. As the threat of climate change steadily increases, planting trees is a fairly simple way city leaders can help stem the negative consequences of rising temperatures and increasing population density.

Subscribe to our channel! http://goo.gl/0bsAjO

Vox.com is a news website that helps you cut through the noise and understand what’s really driving the events in the headlines. Check out http://www.vox.com to get up to speed on everything from Kurdistan to the Kim Kardashian app.

Follow Vox on Twitter: http://goo.gl/XFrZ5H

Transcript
Intro

0:00 · Search Google Earth for China and you’ll see this.

0:03 · But an unedited satellite photo might look more like this.

0:08 · That gray smudge is air pollution and it’s coming from Chinese cars, factories, and power plants.

0:13 · But it’s not only here.

0:14 · In cities around the world, air pollution is a big problem.

0:18 · A majority of humans now live in cities and that number is only going to rise, which means more cars, more factories, and more power plants.

0:25 · As officials explore options for fighting air pollution, there is one tool that is often overlooked: trees.

Air pollution

0:35 · Cities are centers of industry, but the resulting pollution is filling our lungs and making us sick.

0:41 · One major culprit is particulate matter: airborne particles of dust, soot and smoke that are released when we burn fossil fuels or kicked up during construction and farming.

0:49 · When we inhale them, they can cause asthma and they can also enter our bloodstream to cause strokes and even death.

0:54 · Experts estimate that outdoor air pollution kills over three million people a year and as cities grow, leaders are funding creative—and often expensive—solutions for the problem.

1:04 · In London, the mayor spent over a million pounds spraying city streets with an adhesive that was supposed to glue pollutants to the road.

1:10 · and in the Netherlands, designers have created a giant air purifier they call “The Smog Free Tower”, which is cool, but there is another, simpler solution… A new report from The Nature Conservancy shows that planting trees can be a cost-effective way to improve public health, which they do in two ways: First, a tree removes particulate matter when polluted air blows through its branches.

Planting trees

1:31 · The particulate matter settles on the leaves and when it rains the dust is washed down the gutter so we don’t inhale it.

1:37 · Second, trees cool temperatures by providing shade and releasing water through photosynthesis, which cools summer temperatures by about two to four degrees fahrenheit.

1:46 · But there is a catch!

1:47 · Trees can only clean and cool the air within a close radius: about one hundred feet, so city officials need to be careful where they plant.

1:54 · Officials can maximize pollution reduction by planting trees where population density and air pollution overlap.

1:59 · The Nature Conservancy report uses data from Washington D.C. to create a map showing where planting trees will have the highest return on investment.

2:06 · And some trees work better than others: trees with larger, stickier leaves, like maples and elms are more effective, but they also need to be considered within the larger ecosystem.

2:15 · Compared to DC, many cities around the world have even more to gain from planting trees: this map shows where return on investment is highest for reducing particulate matter.

2:23 · With proper targeting, planting trees can be just as cost-effective as other strategies like converting public transportation to use less diesel fuel.

2:30 · But there is one major limiting factor: water access.

2:34 · What might work in Boston, will be less feasible in a city like Doha, Qatar, where water is a scarce resource.

2:39 · And on top of that, many mayors don’t yet think of trees as a public health resource.

2:44 · Trees might not look like giant air filters, but that’s exactly what they are, and the sooner we start thinking of them that way, the sooner the air we breathe might be cooler and cleaner in cities around the world.

Why don’t we just turn empty offices into housing

DW Planet A, 12 01, 2023

Video (12:13)
Description

Many offices are sitting empty following the rise of working from home, while cities around the world face housing crises. Building new housing is extremely carbon intensive. Could converting unused offices into housing help solve both problems?

We’re destroying our environment at an alarming rate. But it doesn’t need to be this way. Our new channel Planet A explores the shift towards an eco-friendly world — and challenges our ideas about what dealing with climate change means. We look at the big and the small: What we can do and how the system needs to change. Every Friday we’ll take a truly global look at how to get us out of this mess.

Credits:

Reporter: Dave Braneck

Camera: Henning Goll

Video Editor: Frederik Willmann

Steven Paynter, Principal Architect, Gensler

Some of the physical challenges of converting offices into housing: https://www.nytimes.com/interactive/2023/03/11/upshot/office-conversions.html

Chapters:

0:51 The Vacancy Problem

2:31 Housing’s environmental Impact

6:55 Improving City Planning

Transcript
Intro

0:00 · If you live in a city, there’s a pretty good chance you’re living through a housing crisis.

0:05 · Rents in major cities are skyrocketing…

0:10 · And this is happening all around the world.

0:15 · At the same time housing has grown scarce, some buildings stand empty.

0:22 · Downtown Frankfurt looks like just about any other big city’s central business district.

0:26 · But the gleaming high-rises do hide an alarming fact — nearly 10% of the city’s offices are vacant.

0:35 · Other major cities around the globe have vacancy rates that high…

0:38 · or even higher.

0:41 · So why don’t we just convert empty offices into housing for people worldwide?

The Vacancy Problem

0:52 · While many of us are back in the office, working from home is here to stay.

0:56 · For instance, 35% of US workers who can work from home still do.

1:01 · And plenty only come into the office on occasion.

1:04 · But vacant workspaces aren’t a new problem.

1:07 · Long before the pandemic, aging offices were already becoming less desirable.

1:11 · “There’s been for about the last 10 years a trend that we called ‘flight to quality.’”

1:17 · Steven Paynter, an architect at one of the world’s biggest firms, focuses on adaptive reuse.

1:22 · “People weren’t renewing their leases in older buildings, they were going to the new ones that were being built, because they offered the kind of amenities and kind of locations people wanted.”

1:32 · All these empty offices aren’t just a waste of space: they mean less rent for owners, lower tax revenue for cities, and the decline of entire neighborhoods.

1:42 · “We have beautiful buildings, we have the wonderful plazas, we have sort of all of the physical assets – we just have vacant buildings.

1:49 · And so you don’t see that vibrancy.

1:51 · You used to, you know, kind of look down these key corridors, and you would see just streams of people, you know, coming down the street, and you don’t see that as much anymore.”

2:00 · Lily Langlois is a planner in San Francisco, another city addressing high vacancies.

2:06 · “Almost 95% of our tax revenue comes from business tax, from downtown.

2:12 · About 80% of our GDP came from downtown companies in 2021.

2:17 · It is our economic engine.

2:19 · And so, it needs to thrive so the city can thrive.”

2:24 · Roughly 1/3rd of offices are vacant in San Francisco – the third-most expensive housing market in the US.

Housing’s environmental Impact

2:31 · At the same time, the ongoing construction of new housing is causing a whole different host of problems.

2:37 · Construction accounts for 13% of global energy-related carbon emissions, more than five times that of the aviation industry.

2:44 · “In order to meet climate targets, but also other sustainability targets, we will need to actually stick with what’s already built.”

2:53 · Pernilla Hagbert researches urban sustainability at Stockholm’s Royal Institute of Technology.

2:59 · “Even if this new production of housing and buildings is done with very energy efficient and optimized technologies, this won’t be enough.

3:10 · We will also need to reduce the total amount of new production.”

3:16 · And this is what brings us to Frankfurt.

3:18 · I checked out an ongoing conversion project – an office tower built in the 90s will soon be reborn as around 150 furnished apartments.

3:28 · Benjamin Albrecht, the developer’s regional European head, is excited about adaptive reuse.

3:50 · But it doesn’t only save on emissions.

3:53 · Revamping an office building can be up to 30 percent cheaper and construction can be done in half the time…

3:59 · Though that depends on the project.

4:00 · This one wasn’t much cheaper than a new build, but speed played a big part.

Adaptive Reuse

4:13 · Repurposing an old building to serve a new function is called adaptive reuse, and can extend a structure’s life.

4:19 · Think of turning old factories into artists’ lofts, or warehouses into ubiquitous street food halls.

4:25 · There are plenty of empty offices, tons of people need housing – it seems like a pretty simple solution.

4:32 · Plus it’s environmentally friendly.

4:33 · So why isn’t this constantly happening?

4:37 · Retrofitting an existing structure is a lot more complicated than planning everything from scratch.

4:43 · As developers have found out, apartments and offices aren’t always a 1-1 fit – it depends on when and where they were built.

4:52 · Modern open-plan offices weren’t built for living in.

4:54 · First, you must divide up large areas while ensuring rooms get enough sunlight.

4:59 · And you can’t just have one big bathroom for a whole floor.

5:03 · Each room needs ventilation, heating and power, too.

5:08 · And for all you know the old building is full of asbestos – not every challenge is clear before renovations start.

5:26 · Steven Paynter, the adaptive reuse specialist, has even developed an algorithm to measure whether offices are good candidates to be reincarnated as housing.

5:36 · “So it looks at say, over 100 different aspects.

5:38 · Some of the key ones are things like the distance between the elevators and the windows.

5:44 · You want your one-bedroom apartment to have a nice bedroom with a window and a nice living space with a window, and then have maybe the kitchen and the bathroom at the back.

5:54 · That’s great.

5:55 · A lot of office buildings actually have way too much space between the elevators and the glazing to make that happen.”

6:02 · This means to end up with shiny new apartments, many conversions essentially rebuild everything except existing foundations and facades.

6:10 · Turns out its just slightly more complicated than just clearing out some cubicles and throwing up a bit of sheetrock – especially if you like luxury amenities like bathrooms in every apartment and windows in every bedroom.

6:23 · These constraints make many offices just too much work to convert.

6:27 · According to Paynter’s research, roughly 30% of offices are ideal candidates.

6:33 · And while not every office building is ripe for conversion, adding up all of those that would be a good fit would still make a massive difference.

6:40 · “If you look at the US market where we’re doing a lot of this work, there’s about 100 billion square feet of office space.

6:47 · And if you’ve converted just the vacancy, or about seven precent of that, you could create between six and seven million new homes.”

Improving City Planning

6:55 · But just turning offices into apartments isn’t going to be enough.

7:00 · Neighborhoods that are just office blocks can be a bit inhospitable – crawling with finance bros by day, morphing into ghost towns outside of business hours.

7:08 · Ensuring people live, not just work there could change that.

7:14 · As we saw for ourselves…

7:18 · This neighborhood of Frankfurt, Niederrad, was a sprawling set of offices in the 60s and 70s with the wildly creative nickname of the ‘Bürostadt’ or office city.

7:28 · But by the mid-2000s, nearly one in three of the offices was sitting empty.

7:43 · In 2006, the city began converting the empty office towers into housing, creating true mixed-use development.

7:50 · Now it’s filled with places to live, shop, eat and go to school, instead of just places to work.

7:58 · Frankfurt rezoned the area, and turned parking lots into green spaces and kindergartens.

8:02 · It brought in developers to build apartments and shops.

8:06 · When they’re done, there’ll be 6,000 apartments here.

8:19 · We took a tour of one of the converted office buildings, a 20-story tower turned into 150 apartments in 2020.

8:26 · Its former life made for some quirks.

8:28 · Every room has sprinklers and architects built around load-bearing beams.

8:32 · But they boasts high ceilings and great views, too.

8:36 · As you can see, many conversions end up as relatively upscale housing.

8:41 · That might take a bit of pressure off the housing market, but it’s not going to bring relief to the masses.

8:45 · If we want this to be more than just some kind of luxury niche we don’t need anything crazy – just a bit of political will.

Cutting Red Tape

8:53 · The Canadian city of Calgary, which started working with Paynter to revitalize its downtown in 2021, is a case in point.

9:01 · “Calgary had about a 38% vacancy in their office market.

9:04 · And that was at the time one of the worst in the world.

9:07 · They very quickly put a program together, which gives you $75 a square foot to convert the building, and moved all of the red tape out of the way to make these projects move more quickly.

9:17 · The first five projects are now under construction.

9:20 · That represents about 750 new homes, and they have 10 more approved.”

9:26 · Paynter says much of this housing will be affordable and built with families in mind, thanks to the financial incentives provided by the city.

9:35 · Adaptive reuse often has even more red tape than new builds – that will have to change if we’re going to use its potential to curb emissions.

9:42 · “If you take Toronto where I am, as an example, there’s a rule in all of downtown that you cannot remove office space: it’s protected as employment lands.

9:51 · That was created in the ’70s, and it just never got updated because there was no need to change it.

9:56 · But now there’s a desperate need to change it and it’s kind of holding up these projects happening.”

10:02 · Such arbitrary regulations are quite common.

10:04 · And approval for conversions often takes as long as it would for a new build, even though the structure’s already in place.

10:11 · Ideally, it’d be the other way around.

10:14 · “In both legislative and sort of from a governance perspective, we need to think of how to actually make it difficult to, for example, tear down a building and build a new building, through zoning and through harder legislation.

10:32 · But also through sort of soft legislation like taxation and financial incentives.”

Potential

10:41 · Frankfurt’s leading the way in Germany, converting offices to nearly 10,000 houses in the last decade.

10:47 · And according to recent studies, there’s room for plenty more in cities all over the map.

10:52 · Frankfurt did it with careful city planning, like in Niederrad.

10:56 · Plus, the more we learn implementing such projects, the greater the savings.

11:01 · Since their first office conversion projects have gone well, Benjamin Albrecht thinks his firm will focus more on adaptive reuse.

11:10 · Many developers have shown interest in these projects, and cities like San Francisco are supporting office conversions.

11:17 · “I don’t think that we’re naive in any way, thinking that we’re going to build hundreds of 1000s of units downtown.

11:23 · But I think it’s, you know, part of the solution.

11:26 · And it is an amazing opportunity, to take advantage of these existing assets.”

11:31 · The kind of support offered may determine the type of housing we get.

11:35 · Flipping offices into housing is clearly no quick fix, and it’s not going to solve the global housing crisis overnight.

11:42 · But Frankfurt and Calgary do show it can make cities vastly more livable and it can drastically reduce the environmental impact of relying solely on new builds.

11:51 · Let us know in the comments if your city has started converting offices to housing.

11:55 · And don’t forget to subscribe, we’ve got new videos for you every Friday.

Why free public transport doesn’t fix traffic (and what does)

DW Planet A, 11 08, 2024

Video (11:19)
Description

If cars are bad for the climate, and public transport good, what makes people switch? From Jakarta to Tallinn, cities are trying to motivate people by eliminating fares. Let’s check if that works.

We’re destroying our environment at an alarming rate. But it doesn’t need to be this way. Planet A explores the shift towards an eco-friendly world — and challenges our ideas about what dealing with climate change means. We look at the big and the small: What we can do and how the system needs to change. Every Friday we’ll take a truly global look at how to get us out of this mess.

Transcript
Intro

0:00 · In 2012, something remarkable happened in the Estonian capital, Tallinn.

0:06 · Cars were congesting the city’s streets - so the local government asked residents: “Do you want free public transport?”

0:13 · They said: “Jah”(Yes).

0:15 · So Tallinn made it free.

0:16 · Since 2013, every resident can just hop on and off trams and buses - without having to buy a ticket.

0:23 · Did this unclog the city?

0:25 · Well…

0:25 · buses were fuller than before.

0:27 · But at the same time, the share of public transit compared to other means of transport dropped.

0:34 · “Ridership has fallen dramatically, from 42% to now like 30%.

0:41 · And in the same time, car use has risen about 5%.”

0:47 · Despite free public transport, people used their cars more.

0:51 · If not money, what does it take to convince people to switch from cars to public transportation?

Why we like driving car

1:01 · “Try to imagine what this country would be like without automobiles.”

1:06 · This General Electric film is from 1948, but our sentiment towards cars hasn’t changed much since.

1:13 · “…there is nothing like a car.”

1:16 · “When people look at the psychological aspects of car ownership, they typically look at areas of status and of pride.”

1:23 · Pete Dyson is a behavioral scientist.

1:26 · He researches why people travel the way they do.

1:29 · “The nature of the tool that it plays in our life provides some freedom to go places and to do things that have some unique aspects.”

1:40 · Cars are about way more than just getting from A to B.

1:43 · They fulfill a lot of what we desire.

1:46 · Take a look at this “pyramid of needs”.

1:49 · It’s a concept from the world of psychology that ranks what we need from basic things like food and safety, to belonging and self-esteem up to self-actualization, so, becoming the best version of yourself.

2:02 · A car covers a whole lot of those needs.

2:05 · Not only does it take you wherever you want to go, whenever you want to go.

2:09 · It’s also relatively safe and comfortable.

2:11 · And friends and colleagues, who see your car as a sign of success, may admire you.

2:17 · Sure, a bus gets you from A to B as well.

2:19 · But forget about glamour or comfort, once you get off a subway, covered in sweat and super late for your business lunch.

2:28 · The thing is just…

2:29 · while driving might be great for those inside the car, it’s actually pretty bad for everyone else.

2:34 · Cars, vans and motorbikes kill around two million people each year - through air pollution or road accidents.

2:42 · Plus, they emit around 10% of global carbon emissions into the air, because most of them still run on fossil fuels.

2:51 · Yes, public transport also emits carbon, but per person it’s much less.

2:57 · Having many people share a vehicle makes the trip more efficient in this respect.

3:02 · That’s why governments want people to switch.

3:04 · After Tallinn, other cities and countries have also tried to bait them with the simplest thing - money - and made public transit free.

3:12 · Like Luxembourg, the island of Malta, Kansas City and Albuquerque.

3:16 · Their experience was largely the same as in Tallinn.

3:20 · “The people who were using public transport anyways, uh, are now using it more often, taking more rides.”

3:27 · Merlin Rehema is a sustainable city researcher based in Tallinn.

3:32 · “To some extent also like short walks and bicycle trips, that were taken before, became then bus trips.”

3:40 · But just very few give up their car.

3:42 · The cost is 40 euros per resident per year to make public transport free in Tallinn.

3:47 · But if buses are packed and not well connected, residents are reluctant to switch.

3:52 · You also need to make the public transport system better.

3:56 · “There are ways in which you can make journeys smoother, more punctual, more reliable by improving, say signaling, giving bus priority to public transport.

4:09 · A safer environment, a more comfortable environment.

4:12 · The access to a seat or a table, or the ability to do useful or meaningful things whilst travelling.”

4:20 · Some cities have understood this and are working hard to improve their public transport system.

4:25 · Like Jakarta, the capital of Indonesia.

Taking the bus in Jakarta

4:29 · There are almost 11 million people there, almost 4 million cars, 17 million motorbikes.

4:36 · People spend hours in traffic.

4:38 · Air pollution has gotten really unhealthy.

4:41 · But the city also has invested a lot in public transport.

4:45 · Here’s what a trip can look like.

4:47 · Within 500 meters of your home, you find a stop for a free minibus that’s part of the “MikroTrans” network.

4:54 · This takes you to a bus station.

4:56 · From there you catch a bus that drives on a separate lane, past all the cars stuck in traffic.

5:01 · The whole trip costs 3,500 Rupiah, as much as a small bottle of water.

5:07 · That’s affordable.

5:09 · The bus is air-conditioned, which is a nice relief.

5:13 · It has service staff on board, and a separate, supposedly safe area for women.

5:18 · These pink busses are women only.

5:22 · BRT, short for Bus Rapid Transit, has become popular in big cities in Latin America and Asia.

5:29 · Jakarta has the largest network, with 14 of these corridors spanning over more than 250 km.

5:36 · They call it “TransJakarta”.

5:38 · The concept of BRT is to give more space to public buses.

5:42 · This way they’re supposed to travel quickly and reliably - without building much new infrastructure.

5:48 · “And it is attractive because it is integrated with MicroTrans, it is integrated with the minibuses or medium buses.

5:57 · We call it first mile and last mile connectivity.”

5:59 · Gonggomtua Sitanggang is the Southeast Asia Director of a non-profit transport institute.

6:05 · He lives in Jakarta and advises the transit operators.

6:09 · The first and last mile connectivity he just spoke about is important to let people access public transport in the first place.

6:16 · That can work with bikes or e-scooters, in Jakarta it’s these minibuses.

6:21 · They used to be informal and privately operated but then got integrated into the public system.

6:28 · 20 years after the first bus corridor was built, Jakarta’s bus network now covers most of the city and is connected to the local train network.

6:39 · Overall about 10% of trips are made by public transport.

6:43 · That might not sound like a lot yet.

6:45 · But the government is thinking big.

6:47 · It wants to increase that number sixfold by 2030.

6:51 · Seems like Jakarta is taking user needs seriously.

6:55 · But they also haven’t found the secret formula yet.

6:58 · Because as public transit has been growing, so has car and motorcycle traffic.

7:04 · It seems that nudging people into public transport is one thing.

7:08 · But…

7:09 · “the major challenge here, or the major homework here, is that to push people to use public transport.”

Making car use harder

7:18 · For decades the solution to too much traffic has been to build more lanes, more roads, more flyovers, more free parking..

7:26 · And Jakarta has done just that.

7:29 · The thing is: that’s not how you unclog your streets.

7:33 · Today we know: More space for cars only attracts more cars.

7:38 · And after a few years, roads are congested again, with more vehicles than before.

7:43 · This is called “induced traffic”.

7:46 · If you want fewer cars, you’re better off doing the opposite: make it harder and more inconvenient to drive a car.

7:55 · Again, the most obvious tool is money.

7:58 · From 2025, car owners in Estonia will have to pay two kinds of taxes.

8:04 · One when they buy a car and one when they use it.

8:07 · They aren’t big fans of that new tax, of course.

8:11 · London has established a congestion charge zone.

8:14 · Everyone driving into it during the day has to pay 15 pounds.

8:19 · Car traffic dropped significantly, while bus and metro use went up.

8:25 · But simply making it expensive to drive might hit poorer people particularly hard.

8:30 · There are other options too.

8:32 · “They can also be like non-monetary measures, like really redesigning your cities in order to favor public transport use and, and active modes.”

8:45 · Paris for example has been removing tens of thousands of parking lots, closing entire roads to cars and tripling parking fees for big and polluting SUVs.

8:54 · Jakarta is starting to redesign as well in an area with tens of thousands of parking spaces.

9:01 · Gongomtua Sitangang is advising the pilot project.

9:06 · “We start by improving the connectivity, the pedestrian facilities and also cycling facilities, and then we also develop a strategy of how to reduce the parking space within the area.

9:22 · What is important here is that the neighborhood sits at a major public transport hub with bus and rail connections.

9:28 · You can only put pressure on car drivers if you have good public transport alternatives ready.

What now?

9:35 · Just like with cars, people have very different needs and expectations when it comes to good public transport.

9:41 · It must be…

9:42 · “easy and enjoyable” “in between affordable and accessible” “Reliable and comfortable” And just like that, each city starts from a different point.

9:54 · In terms of traffic, infrastructure, budget, mobility behavior, and so on.

10:00 · There’s no single formula for public transport that applies to everyone.

10:04 · Whether you’re “nudging” or “pushing” residents into transit, it still looks different in each place.

10:09 · But the goal is the same: to reduce traffic, carbon emissions and air pollution.

10:16 · The challenge is that new public transit has to be built within existing cities, in between houses, cars and people.

10:23 · Unless you want chaos and frustrated citizens, room for private vehicles can only be reduced step by step as well.

10:31 · And we haven’t even talked about rural areas yet, where routes are longer and buses scarcer.

10:36 · But a few quick fixes could make public transport more convenient almost immediately.

10:42 · Like an app that shows in real-time where the bus is and when it’ll arrive.

10:47 · Or a uniform, maybe cheap fare system that works for different types of transit.

10:52 · Little things that reduce stress and give a sense of control.

10:59 · What do you think about buses or trains where you are?

11:02 · Leave us a comment and subscribe, we post a new video every Friday.

Why It’s Usually Hotter In A City Let’s Talk NPR

NPR, 08 14, 2018

Description

The way cities are designed and inhabited can make actually make them hotter. So what’s the solution? We explore a few ideas for beating the urban heat.

Transcript

0:04 · There’s a reason that summers in the city are so hot, and it’s probably easiest to explain why with thermal vision.

0:11 · It shows the heat all around me and also the coldness of this ice cream cone.

0:16 · Cities are often hotter than their suburbs due to a phenomenon known as “the heat island effect.”

The Heat Island Effect

0:24 · All of that asphalt, concrete, dark rooftops and tall buildings— they absorb and store heat.

0:31 · And there are a lot of people using a lot of energy— driving cars, riding subways, running the AC.

0:38 · All of these materials and activities either create or retain heat.

0:44 · And all that stored heat causes steeper nighttime highs … when people would otherwise have a chance to cool down.

0:52 · The way all these heat-storing elements are laid out matters quite a bit.

0:57 · In cities laid out in grids, like Phoenix, Chicago, or Washington, D.C., the buildings are closer together, like closely packed coals in a fire, keeping heat in.

1:07 · More space between buildings creates more circulation.

1:11 · Another way to combat this heat island effect is through green space.

1:15 · Parks, trees, plants— they don’t absorb as much heat … they actually send water back in the atmosphere.

1:21 · Between all those hot buildings, that cools things off.

1:25 · Another solution is color.

Color Light

1:27 · Light colors reflect sunlight back into the atmosphere instead of storing it as heat.

1:31 · It’s easiest to see in crosswalks, and it’s true for the color of buildings and other urban surfaces, too.

1:39 · And one long-game way to combat the heat island effect is to avoid using air conditioning when you don’t need it.

Avoid Using Air Conditioning

1:45 · AC uses a ton of electricity and creates a feedback loop.

1:49 · When we get that electricity from coal or natural gas, that puts more carbon dioxide into the atmosphere … which warms the planet … making cities even hotter.

2:00 · But this isn’t just about comfort.

2:02 · Today, heat waves kill more people than any other extreme weather event.

2:07 · More than tornadoes, hurricanes and even floods.

2:11 · City populations keep growing, and those cities are only getting hotter.

2:17 · This is Let’s Talk, NPR’s news explainer show.

2:20 · Make sure to subscribe to our YouTube channel and check out other shows.

2:25 · I’m Christopher Joyce, and this is NPR.

Why the Nepal flash flood was almost impossible to predict

CBC News, 09 02, 2026

Video (11:52)
Description

A collapsed glacier in Nepal caused catastrophic flooding that killed more than a thousand people, while thousands more are still missing. Andrew Chang breaks down what accelerated the flood to such a violent degree and how factors like the scale of the Himalayan mountains and the region’s climate make prediction and monitoring so challenging.

Transcript
Intro

0:00 · If you ever needed convincing that minutes matter, this was it.

0:04 · Quick thinking on courage saved the lives of over 1,600 school children [music] as devastating flash floods hit Nepal on Wednesday.

0:11 · A wall of earth, ice, and water comes crashing down a mountain thousands of meters high. All that debris and flash flooding barreling through the valleys of Nepal, and this school is about to be wiped off the map.

0:25 · An entire river valley along the Nepal border wiped out in seconds.

0:29 · Because absolutely everything [music] is getting destroyed.

0:33 · A monster wave crashing down from the mountains sweeping away everything in its path.

0:38 · Entire towns washed away.

0:45 · But this school, which was also destroyed, best as I can tell, not one student died.

0:52 · I was inside my classroom.

0:53 · One of my colleagues went around and came here, and he said that the flood is coming, the flood is coming. All areas washed away.

1:01 · The principal you just heard figures he had about 13, 14 minutes to get nearly a thousand students out of school and to higher ground. [music] In the chaos of the evacuation, he even had the foresight to reroute buses full of [music] younger children that were on their way to school, telling them, “Don’t come. Get somewhere safe.” This is one incredible outcome in a disaster full of terrible ones, but it also raises a question.

1:28 · If someone knew this flood was coming, why didn’t everyone get that call?

A cascading disaster

1:39 · Even if you knew nothing about Nepal, I could situate this for you by saying this disaster happened about a hundred or so kilometers west of Mount Everest in the Himalayas. [music] And when we think about floods in this part of the world, especially during monsoon season, which [music] it is now, we’re often thinking torrential downpours of rain. That’s not what this was.

2:00 · What we really saw was that the the lower part of a tongue of a glacier completely sheared off across a width of 1,300 m and it’s really like someone took a knife and basically cut the lower part of a glacier and dropped it off the mountain cliff.

2:14 · Yes, this wasn’t initially water, but rather the earth and its ice that came crashing down. It’s here on the northern face of the Langtang Range where this massive glacier is believed to have collapsed, detaching from the mountainside more than 5,000 m above sea level, sending an enormous amount of ice, rock, force, and energy screaming thousands of kilometers down a steep slope.

2:40 · The avalanche would ultimately tear through the twists and turns of the valley, slamming into a river, sending an immense amount of water, mud, and debris into nearby Rasuwagadhi, a gateway between Nepal and China, destroying it in seconds. The flash flood continued south, gathering more water, scarring the riverbanks, swallowing entire settlements along the way. Timure and surrounding towns basically [music] disappeared.

3:09 · Further south, Sabrubesi, it was considered a hub of the region. Thousands lived on either side of the river and tourists came here, too. If you wanted to trek through Nepal’s mountainside, you might start here. But the whole area absolutely ravaged. Dams, buildings, highways, bridges washed away. And the destruction this flood carved through the country would eventually reach more than 100 km away from where it started.

3:41 · It all happened very quickly, too quickly. There are warning systems in place, but they’re not really designed to track this kind of disaster.

3:50 · So, one of the only monitoring systems that we have in place are river gauges that that can track when there’s a sudden increase in the water level. That can trigger a siren downstream that can hopefully get to the communities that need it in time for them to evacuate.

4:10 · Water [music] is a major threat in these mountain ranges, but flooding is usually driven by rain or by melt, things you can track in real time. So, river and lake levels, [music] for example, can rise during monsoon season, or you can have glacial meltwater pool and then burst its banks.

4:30 · Point is, it tends to happen gradually in a way that these river gauges can actually measure over time and then [music] relay that data downstream to where it’s useful. Except in this case, it would have been like measuring the direction of a hurricane by holding out a finger. The speed of the flood was so overwhelming, the sudden flow so catastrophic, in some cases it destroyed the monitoring systems before they could even send their alerts.

4:59 · So, we went from, you know, real-time data to all of a sudden there was no information.

5:06 · So, how do you warn people about a disaster that moves faster than the warning system itself?

A blind spot?

5:16 · [music] The thing is, there’s a lot we understand about how these disasters happen.

5:23 · This is absolutely an event of a type that occurs in the Himalaya. It has occurred many times in recent recorded history.

5:31 · And even in those cases where people are in danger, there are success stories, like in Switzerland. Now, don’t get me wrong, what happened in the small Swiss mountain town of Blattem last year was tragic.

5:45 · [music] An entire community was buried under an avalanche of ice and rock.

5:49 · Authorities saying 90% of the village of Blatten has been covered by thousands of tons of ice, [music] mud, and rock.

5:56 · But nearly every single person survived because the town had been evacuated [music] more than a week earlier. Hundreds of people fleeing the area well in advance because of what locals and scientists had been noticing for years.

6:13 · In the European Alps, uh the mountainside collapse in Blatten, Switzerland, uh a year or so ago, uh that was known to be an issue well before it happened.

6:24 · These are photos from a Swiss natural hazard monitoring company showing radar and camera setups pointed directly at the [music] mountain, which they had believed at the time might give way at any moment. This type of precise [music] measuring equipment can reveal even the tiniest details about a mountain, its cracks, its movement, the slightest [music] changes in position. And what that equipment detected was this.

6:48 · What you’re seeing here is a 26-hour time-lapse [music] from the same Swiss company, GeoPrevent, just before the glacier collapse. Maybe seemingly slow, but alarmingly fast [music] for a mountain. The Earth was moving 10 m a day.

7:09 · With this kind of information, you [music] can’t predict the exact timing of the collapse itself. But this kind of observation is what allowed scientists to recognize [music] the increasing chance that something bad might happen at some point. The danger [music] was progressive and predictable.

7:27 · It’s easier to access some of those cliff sides in Switzerland, get sensors and monitoring equipment in those locations so that we can keep get real-time data. Here in Nepal, um the extreme elevations we’re looking at, there’s just there’s no trail to the top, there’s not a cable car there.

7:43 · The capacity and the resources necessary are going to be very difficult to replicate in places like the Himalayas.

7:50 · It’s one thing when you’ve been noticing changes over a long period and you decide to point a whole bunch of measuring devices at a very specific spot. But the problem with the Himalayas is scale.

8:01 · The physical scale of the range, the difficulty in working with it, the fact that it’s often covered in clouds as it is due to the monsoon season now, it it presents tremendous challenge for prediction and monitoring.

8:14 · This part of the world isn’t just a mountain range. It is an entire wall of mountains stretching thousands of kilometers containing some of the highest peaks and deepest valleys on Earth.

8:28 · Glaciers and the lakes they melt into are a key concern because they can overflow. But there are thousands of them across Asia and [music] scientists do their best to track every single one with reports like this one from 2020 creating a meticulous inventory of the most dangerous sites like one giant catalog of risk. There’s promise there, maybe, you think. And looking in hindsight at what happened in Nepal, they are indeed finding clues that suggest maybe these types of events are predictable.

9:02 · A new report published after the collapse refers to how movement of the ice surface sped up just prior, how meltwater had turned visibly brown suggesting mountain sediment was shifting. Also, noticing a crack propagating in the days leading up to the disaster. Even learning to better track events as they occur, noticing that the mass movement caused a seismic signal at a magnitude larger than four. So, maybe there’s promise in deploying more seismic sensors.

9:33 · And imagine tying all that knowledge together, pushing it to the limit using AI, combining susceptibility maps [music] with real-time data to inch closer and closer to predicting the unpredictable. But then you remember, once again, the problem of scale. I think one Washington-based think tank put [music] it best. The Himalayan region offers more slopes capable of failing at this magnitude [music] across greater vertical relief than anywhere else on Earth.

10:04 · Then it goes on to this point of how hard it can be to gauge risk even when you think you’ve found it. Of roughly a dozen extreme flow events in the region in the [music] past 10 years, only one originated in a place already known to be a risk. Every other one of these events also came from somewhere no one was watching.

10:26 · It’s very challenging to predict exactly where and when these rock collapses are going to occur.

10:33 · It would be terrific if you could put sensors in in all the locations where events like this potentially were going to happen, but it’s just not practical to monitor so many different locations all at once. As remote sensing technologies improve, as computing improves, we’ll be more and more able to watch the landscape and detect small amounts of change, but that’s still a work in progress.

11:01 · And that’s really the key to all of this. Right now, there is no crystal ball, just small incremental ways scientists are learning how to buy precious seconds, maybe even minutes. But trying to prevent or even predict the inevitable, National Geographic referred to these glaciers as cryogenic time bombs, a reference to our changing climate and how frozen water that makes up this kind of glue that binds the Earth together, the cryosphere, it’s melting.

11:33 · Is that to blame in Nepal?

11:36 · We may never know directly, but it is why scientists [music] are so certain something like this will happen again. It’s just a question of when and where.

11:46 · [music]

Why the planet is drying out much faster than before, according to a new study

According to a new study, the planet is drying at an unprecedented pace, presenting a critical threat to humanity. Transcript

0:02 · Ali: The planet is drying at an unprecedented pace, presenting a critical threat to humanity.

0:07 · That’s according to a new study publish understand in the journal “Science advances.”

0:12 · Researchers found that continental drying is having profound global impact that threaten water availability across the globe.

0:19 · For part of our tipping point coverage, I spoke recently with a climate investigations editor at pro publica, and began by asking him why so much of the Earth is drying out.

0:30 · It’s a mixture of climate change and massive overuse of water resources by people that appears to be drying out a continent, according to a new study out of Arizona state university. And it basically found that rising temperatures are leading to less precipitation in certain places and faster evaporation from both the soil and rivers and surface waters. At the same time that overpumping of ground water aquifers is quickly depleting resources held in those underground reservoirs.

1:02 · Ali: Who’s driving this problem?

1:03 · It’s difficult to parse out the difference between climate change as an effect and the human overpumping of ground water, but those are the two factors. Of course, it’s the I missions from the burning of fossil fuels that is driving the rising temperatures on the planet, and so that is really contributing to a water loss or a water reportioning across the globe.

1:25 · And the ground water pumping is driven by individual use, by farmers, by corporations, by cities around the world. It is becoming the fallback kind of bank account, if you will, of water resources that people in groups are turning to as their surface waters are diminished or as droughts deplete the waters for a shorter period of time. People are looking to those underground reservoirs and pumping more, pumping faster, exploring deeper.

1:51 · Ali: And the study uses nasa satellite images over the course of 20 years to track the evolution of land that’s drying out.

1:59 · Can you walk us through mechanically how that works, how the satellite images were able to capture this change?

2:05 · It essentially measures changing mass in the spinning Earth, changes in the Earth’s rotational orbit. So it can measure how much volume is in the poles and how much water is underground, and changes in that mass. And over the last 20 years, it’s allowed researchers to be able to tell the difference between one type of changing mass and another.

2:28 · So now really for the first time these researchers are using that gray satellite data to get a sense of the Earth’s total water supply and then to distinguish between how much of the loss of that water supply is for melting glaciers from climate change, for example, and how much is from something like ground water depletion.

2:46 · Ali: What are we talking about here in terms of total water loss, and also, how has this accelerated over the past 20 years?

2:54 · Researchers caution they don’t know where the bottom is, so to speak. So we don’t have a measure of how much fresh water there is to work with. What we do know is that the accounts that we’re working with are quickly being withdrawn. So we can see the rate of drying that they measured has quickly accelerated, just over the last 10 years or so, since 2014. They’ve been measuring for 23 years.

3:19 · They’ve seen that rapid depletion from the very beginning. But it has intensified and accelerated just in the last 10 or 12 years. So that’s particularly alarming. It’s something that led them to write in the paper that this issue is a critical and emerging threat to humanity.

3:35 · Ali: What do we know about where this is effecting the globe?

3:40 · Is it happening more in certain parts of the globe, or is it pretty evenly distributed?

3:44 · The study found that drying places are getting dryer faster than wedding places are getting wetter, and it found those drying regions are much, much larger than the places that are getting increasingly welt. And those drying regions are spread across every continent. There’s substantial drying regions, which researchers now describe as mega regions that constitute much of the southern United States and into Mexico and Central America.

4:10 · There’s a mega drying region that they identified that covered pretty much the whole of Europe and into the Middle East and includes parts of north Africa and again a large swat of south Asia is also part of these mega drying regions. And what those mega regions constitute is the merging of places that already were known to be losing water or suffering from drought, but they used to be finite or distinct places.

4:34 · We used to think, for example, of the southwestern united States as being particularly dry, which it is, and suffering from climate change, which it is. But until recently, we didn’t understand that that drying region was spreading across the whole southern part of the country and is extending down into Mexico and into central America, and that it’s becoming one large drought plagued or drying out region.

4:58 · Ali: How much are we able to connect this phenomenon we’re seeing around the world with the impact on the inhabitants who live in these parts of the world?

5:06 · Are we seeing this play out in real-world implications right now?

5:11 · Yeah, absolutely.

5:13 · The water loss that this research identified is mainly occurring in mid latitude regions, which is where most of the population of the planet resides, and the researchers identified water net loss in 101 countries, and those 101 countries happen to be home to about 75% of the planet’s population. So about six billion people live in the regions that are on balance losing access to fresh water. Ali: The thank you so much.

5:45 · Thank you.

World Weather Attribution — understanding climate change and extreme weather

World Weather Attribution, 11 13, 2023

Video (5:59)
Description

When an extreme weather event occurs, people often ask: is climate change to blame?

For decades, climate scientists have answered that question in general terms – that as the planet warms, we can expect many of these weather events to become more frequent and extreme.

But in recent years, advances in attribution science have allowed researchers to answer the question in much greater detail.

Founded in 2015 by Dr. Friederike Otto and Dr. Geert Jan van Oldenborgh, World Weather Attribution scientists quantify how climate change influences the intensity and likelihood of an extreme weather event. They often do this using weather data and computer modelling, and often immediately in the aftermath of the extreme event.

To encourage actions that will make communities and countries more resilient to future extreme weather events, World Weather Attribution studies also evaluate how existing vulnerability worsened the impacts of the extreme weather event.

To learn more about World Weather Attribution or to view our studies, go to https://www.worldweatherattribution.org/

Follow us on Twitter: https://twitter.com/WWAttribution

Film produced by Geoff Marsh.

The urban configration

21:43 · The building itself, the structure can sustain a longer ignition compared to a tree. A tree can burn for, I dunno, a minute or two, a structure can burn for an hour.

21:53 · It’s the contents of those homes and businesses because that becomes fuel. And now we’re producing very large long burning embers that can fly with the wind to ignite.

22:05 · When we have dense neighborhoods, we have more cars, we have more fences, we have more sheds.

22:09 · Now we have two factors that combine to make fires unstoppable. Ember cast and structure to structure ignition. The priority was rescue. So we’re gonna take care of life first and then save property as we can. The conditions were so bad that I couldn’t see the end of my hood. 23:01 · It was a a a, a middle aged lady and she says my dad is trapped in the, in the house. He can’t get out. So we immediately ran up to the house, grabbed him, got him out to my vehicle, and our fire personnel and our law enforcement personnel went on calls for service like that all through the night While assisting with evacuations. in Pacific Palisades was the first priority for Keegan and the community fire brigade.

23:50 · Somebody died on PCH, somebody died up Las Flores, somebody died in Big Rock.
